K ​ K KK - and E E -theory via homotopy theory
Ulrich Bunke

Abstract
We provide a homotopy theorist’s point of view on K ​ K KK -and E E -theory for C ∗ C^{*} -algebras. We construct stable ∞ \infty -categories representing these theories through a sequence of Dwyer-Kan localizations of the category of C ∗ C^{*} -algebras. Thereby we will reveal the homotopic theoretic meaning of various classical constructions from C ∗ C^{*} -algebra theory, in particular of Cuntz’ q q -construction. We will also discuss operator algebra K K -theory in this framework.



1
Introduction

In this note we describe a construction of stable
\infty
-categories representing
KK
- and
E
-theory for
C^{*}
-algebras through sequences of localizations of the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
of separable
C^{*}
-algebras followed by a left Kan-extension along the inclusion of separable into all
C^{*}
-algebras.
In contrast to the previous constructions of such an
\infty
-category
[
LN18
]
,
[
BEL
]
in the case of
KK
-theory, the description presented here is independent of the classical group-valued
KK
-theory introduced
[
Kas88
]
,
[
Cun87
]
which is described e.g. in the textbooks
[
Bla98
]
,
[
Hig90b
]
. A stable
\infty
-category representing
E
-theory
has not been considered so far.

The main goal of this note is to give a complete account of the basic categorical and functorial properties of
KK
- and
E
-theory using only the basic elements of
C^{*}
-algebra theory. In this way we hope to make these theories more accessible to readers with a homotopy theory background.
The approach to
KK
- and
E
-theory described here can easily be generalized to the case of
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras for discrete groups
G
(see e.g.
[
BD24
]
for
E
-theory) or to
C_{0}(X)
-algebras. With more modifications it should be possible to develop a similar approach to the algebraic version of
KK
-theory
[
CT07
]
,
[
Gar14
]
,
[
Gar16
]
,
[
Ell14
]
. It is also an interesting task to provide a homotopy theoretic interpretation of the constructions from
[
Cun98
]
in the spirit of the present paper.

The starting point of our construction is the characterization of the stable
\infty
-category version of
KK
-theory through a universal property.

Definition 1.1
.

The functor
\mathrm{kk}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{KK}
is initial for functors from
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
to cocomplete stable
\infty
-categories
which are homotopy invariant, stable, semiexact and
s
-finitary.

This means that
\mathrm{kk}
has these properties, described in detail in
2.4
, and that for any cocomplete stable
\infty
-category

{\mathbf{D}}
the restriction along
\mathrm{kk}
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{kk}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathrm{KK},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,se,sfin}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})\ .

Here the superscripts
colim
and
h,s,se,sfin
stand for colimit-preserving and the corresponding properties listed in
1.1
.

The characterization of
KK
-theory by
1.1
was given in
[
BEL
]
following
[
LN18
]
. A similar characterization of the group-valued
KK
-functor through universal properties has been known for a long time
[
Hig88
]
.

The characterization of the stable
\infty
-category representing
E
-theory is similar and obtained by replacing in
1.1
the condition of semiexactness by exactness. The motivation comes from the universal property of the classical
E
-theory stated in
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.6]
.

Definition 1.2
.

The functor
\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}
is initial for functors from
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
to cocomplete stable
\infty
-categories
which are homotopy invariant, stable, exact and
s
-finitary.

In this case we have an equivalence

\mathrm{e}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathrm{E},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,ex,sfin}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})\ .

Our construction proceeds with the following steps which are designed to force the universal properties stated above:

1.

L_{h}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is a Dwyer-Kan localization which inverts the homotopy equivalences.
The resulting
\infty
-category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is left-exact (see
Section

3
) and the functor
L_{h}
is
Schochet exact in the sense that it sends Schochet fibrant cartesian squares to cartesian squares.

2.

L_{K}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}\to L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is a smashing left Bousfield localization which inverts the left-upper corner inclusions and produces a semi-additive and left-exact
\infty
-category (see
Section

4
).

3.

We restrict to the full subcategory of separable algebras and form a left-exact Dwyer-Kan localization

L_{\mathrm{sep},!}:L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}\to L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,!}
for
!
in
\{\mathrm{splt},\mathrm{se},\mathrm{ex}\}
which forces split exact, semi-split exact or exact sequences to induce fibre sequence (see
Section

5
).

4.

For
!
in
\{\mathrm{se},\mathrm{ex}\}
the two-fold loop functor
\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{sep},!}:L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,!}\to L_{K}{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,!}}^{\mathrm{group}}
(where
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{group}}
denotes the full subcategory of group objects in a semi-additive
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
) turns out to be the right-adjoint of a right Bousfield localization and has a stable target.
The composition of the localizations above give functors

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}

\displaystyle:=L_{K}{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}}^{\mathrm{group}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}

\displaystyle:=L_{K}{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{ex}}}^{\mathrm{group}}\ .

(see
Section

7
).

5.

We define the presentable stable
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{KK}
and
\mathrm{E}
as the
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-completions of the stable
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}

and the functors

\mathrm{kk}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{KK}\ ,\quad\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}

by left Kan-extending the compositions

C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{y}\mathrm{KK}\ ,\quad\mbox{or}\quad C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{y}\mathrm{E}

along the inclusion of separable
C^{*}
-algebras into all
C^{*}
-algebras (see
Section

8
).
Theorem

8.5
states that the functors constructed by this procedure indeed satisfy the conditions of
1.1
and
1.2
.

One interesting consequence of the constructions is that the functors

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ ,\>\>\>\quad\mbox{and}\>\>\>\quad\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}

are Dwyer-Kan localizations (see
7.5
).

Our construction of
KK
- and
E
-theory for separable
C^{*}
-algebras via a sequence of localizations is analogous to the construction of an additive category representing
E
-theory in
[
Hig90a
]
. The idea of left-Kan extending
KK
-theory from separable
C^{*}
-algebras to all
C^{*}
-algebras also appears in
[
Ska88
]
.

The category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
has symmetric monoidal structures
\otimes_{\max}
and
\otimes_{\min}
.
The
\mathrm{kk}
- and
\mathrm{e}
-theory functors have symmetric monoidal refinements which are characterized by
symmetric monoidal versions of
1.1
and
1.2
. We will discuss the universal properties of the
symmetric monoidal refinements in the main body of the present paper.

The categories
\mathrm{KK}
and
\mathrm{E}
whose construction is sketched above are stable
\infty
-categories. For any two

C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
we therefore have mapping spectra

\mathrm{KK}(A,B):={\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{KK}}(\mathrm{kk}(A),\mathrm{kk}(B))\ ,\quad\mathrm{E}(A,B):={\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{e}(A),\mathrm{e}(B))\ .

Taking homotopy groups we get
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
KK
- and
E
-theory groups

\mathrm{KK}_{*}(A,B):=\pi_{*}\mathrm{KK}(A,B)\ ,\quad\mathrm{E}_{*}(A,B):=\pi_{*}\mathrm{E}(A.B)\ .

The approach to
KK
- and
E
-theory taken in the present note turns the classical constructions
of these group-valued bifunctors into calculations. Our homotopy theoretic construction of

KK
- and
E
- theory is straightforward once one knows which universal property one would like to enforce.
Composition, homotopy invariance, stability and the respective exactness properties come for free. Also Bott periodicity is just a property which holds because of the existence of the Toeplitz extension.
The real problem in our approach is to calculate the homotopy groups of the mapping spaces in order to see that they
coincide with the classical groups. The latter are defined in terms of Kasparov modules (see
[
Cun87
]
,
[
Cun98
]

[
Tho99
]
,
[
DWW18
]
for alternatives) in the case of
KK
-theory, or in case of
E
-theory,
by the one-categorical localization procedure as in
[
Hig90a
]
or asymptotic morphisms
[
CH90
]
.
The comparison of the homotopy groups of the mapping spectra of the categories constructed in the present note with the classical groups is not obvious at all just from the construction. But it is crucial if one wants
to use the models proposed in this note as a homotopy theoretic replacement of the classical analytic constructions.

In the case of
KK
-theory one could argue by a comparison of
the universal properties that the functors
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
for separable algebras
constructed in the present paper and in
[
LN18
]
are canonically equivalent. Moreover, in

[
BEL
]
we have shown that
the composition

C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ho}}\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}

is equivalent to
the triangulated category valued
KK
-theory of
[
MN06
]
, and that the
KK
-groups

\mathrm{KK}_{0}(A,B)
for separable
C^{*}
-algebras
A,B
are canonically isomorphic to the
KK
-groups introduced in
[
Kas88
]
. But this argument has a draw back.
Though the classical definition of

KK
-groups in terms of equivalence classes of Kasparov modules is not very complicated,
this method of comparison also relies on
the construction of the composition (i.e., the Kasparov product) and the verification of semiexactness
in the classical theory which
are deep theorems.
It is therefore one of the guiding challenges of the present paper to give an independent complete proof for the comparison.

From the perspective of the present notes it is natural to compare the
KK
- and
E
-theory functors
of the present paper with the classical ones by comparing their universal properties. This can be done in a model independent way by defining
the classical functors

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}\ ,\quad\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}

as the universal homotopy invariant, stable and split-exact or half-exact functors, respectively, to an additive category in the sense of
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]
or
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.6]
.
These can directly be compared with the compositions

\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ho}}\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ ,\quad\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ho}}\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ .

The following is a consequence of
Theorem

13.16

and the automatic semiexactness theorem
Theorem

12.1
(which allows to replace
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
appearing in
Theorem

13.16
) by
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
).

Corollary 1.3
.

We have commutative squares

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.88391pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.37016pt\raise-8.41664pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

where the dashed arrows are equivalences of additive categories.

One could argue that in the case of
KK
-theory the proof of
1.3
has
a similar problem as the argument mentioned above since
we must know that our preferred model
of
\mathrm{kk}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
has the universal property stated in
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]
.
We therefore will provide another, completely independent comparison with the Cuntz picture of
KK
by showing the formula (
1.4
) below.
One could then read the arguments also in a different direction as showing that the Cuntz model
indeed has the universal property
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]
.

In our approach the enrichment of
KK
- and
E
-theory theory in spectra is a natural consequence of the stability of the
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{KK}
or
\mathrm{E}
. But point-set level constructions of spectral enrichments of
KK
-theory have previously been considered in

[
JS09
]
,
[
Mit
]
.

As can be seen from the description above our approach to a stable
\infty
-category representing
KK
- and
E
- theory is different from other attempts to produce such stable
\infty
-categories which were guided by the methods of motivic homotopy theory
[
Oes10
]
,
[
Mah14
]
.
There the idea was to start from the category of presheaves
{\mathbf{Fun}}((C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Spc})
, to
perform a series of left Bousfield localizations forcing homotopy invariance,
stability and the desired version of exactness
!
in
\{\mathrm{splt},\mathrm{se},\mathrm{ex}\}
, and finally to apply
-\otimes\mathbf{Sp}
in presentable
\infty
-categories in order to stablize.
Let us denote the resulting presentable stable
\infty
-category by
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
. It comes with a functor
\mathit{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
which by construction has the universal property that

\mathit{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{sep},!},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,!}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})

for any presentable stable
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
. The main non-trivial question is then to understand the relation between
\pi_{*}{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{sep},!}}(\mathit{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}(A),\mathit{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}(B))
and the classical
KK
-groups
\mathrm{KK}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep},*}(A,B)
(for
!=\mathrm{se}
) or
E
-theory groups
\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep},*}(A,B)
(for
!=\mathrm{ex}
). We will not pursue this direction.

As said above the advantage of the constructions in the present note is that they do not require previous knowledge of
KK
- or
E
-theory. In contrast, in
[
LN18
]
,
[
BEL
]
the basic idea was to construct the category
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
as a Dwyer-Kan localization of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
at the
\mathrm{kk}
-equivalences. The latter notion was imported from the classical theory.
In the present paper we do not have to know from the beginning what a
\mathrm{kk}
-equivalence is. The notion of a
\mathrm{kk}
-equivalence comes out at the end as a morphism which is sent to an equivalence by the functor
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
. The input for the construction of
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
in the present paper are only simple
C^{*}
-algebraic notions as homotopy of homomorphisms, compact operators and semi-split exact sequences.

The construction of the
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{KK}
and
\mathrm{E}
via localizations and
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-completions is very suitable for understanding functors out of these categories. This will be employed in some subsequent papers. On the other hand, it is notoriously difficult to understand the homotopy types of the mapping spaces in a Dwyer-Kan localization just from the definition. In
Section

9
and
Section

10
we will,
with some effort, calculate the mapping spectra
\mathrm{E}(A,B)
for
A\cong{\mathbb{C}}
or
A\cong S({\mathbb{C}})\cong C_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
explicitly.

We first define the commutative ring spectrum
\mathrm{KU}:=\mathrm{E}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})
. We justify this notation by providing a ring isomorphism

\pi_{*}\mathrm{KU}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[b,b^{-1}]
with
\deg(b)=-2
and comparing
\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{KU}
with the classical constructions of an infinite loop space with the same name.
Since
\mathrm{e}({\mathbb{C}})
is the tensor unit of
\mathrm{E}
this category has a canonical enrichment over the category
{\mathbf{Mod}}(\mathrm{KU})
of
\mathrm{KU}
-module spectra.
In
9.3
we then define the lax symmetric monoidal
{\mathbf{Mod}}(\mathrm{KU})
-valued
K
-theory functor for
C^{*}
-algebras simply as

K(-):=\mathrm{E}({\mathbb{C}},-):C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(\mathrm{KU})\ .

(1.1)

This gives an effordless construction of a highly structured version of a
K
-theory functor for
C^{*}
-algebras.
For previous constructions of spectrum-valued
K
-theory functors see e.g.
[
BJS03
]
,
[
DEKM11
]
,
[
Joa03
]
,

[
DP15
]
.

Recall that the classical constructions of
C^{*}
-algebra
K
-theory groups as described e.g. in
[
Bla98
]
employ
equivalence classes of projections or components of unitary groups.
In order to connect our definition (
1.1
) with the classical ones and in order to
show that it gives the correct group valued functors after taking homotopy groups we
relate the infinite loop space valued functor
\Omega^{\infty}K
with spaces of projections or unitaries. Thereby we take care of the natural commutative monoid or groups structures.

Using that
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is semi-additive we can define the commutative monoid (see
4.8
)

\mathcal{P}\mathrm{roj}^{s}(B):={\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}}({\mathbb{C}},B)

of stable projections and the commutative group (see
4.9
.)

{\mathcal{U}}^{s}(B):={\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}}(S({\mathbb{C}}),B)

of stable unitaries in
B
. The following result combines
9.4
and
10.8
.

Corollary 1.4
.

1.

If
B
is unital, then
there is a canonical morphism
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{roj}^{s}(B)\to\Omega^{\infty}K(B)
in
\mathbf{CMon}(\mathbf{Spc})

which presents its target as the group completion.

2.

We have a canonical equivalence
{\mathcal{U}}^{s}(B)\simeq\Omega^{\infty-1}K(B)
in
\mathbf{CGroups}(\mathbf{Spc})
.

The canonical morphisms in
1.4
are induced by the Steps
3
to
5
of the above sequence of localizations.
The standard modification of
1.4
.
1
for non-unital
C^{*}
-algebras will be stated as
Theorem

10.7
.

If one goes over to connected components in Assertion
1
or homotopy groups in
Assertion
2
, and if one interprets
\pi_{*}K(B)
as the classical version of
K
-theory of
C^{*}
-algebras, then the assertions of
1.4
are well-known.
The main point of
1.4
is that
K(B)
is not given by the classical definitions but is defined through mapping spectra of
the category
\mathrm{E}
which is constructed by a formal homotopy theoretic procedure of
Dwyer-Kan localizations. It is only by
1.4
that we know that these mapping spectra have the correct homotopy types to represent the classical
K
-theory of
C^{*}
-algebras.

An advantage of the definition of the
K
-theory functor for
C^{*}
-algebras by (
1.1
)
is that it
is homotopy invariant, stable, exact, and also
s
-finitary by construction. In addition,
in
9.7
we show, using the equivalence from
1.4
.
2
,
that it also preserves filtered colimits. Of course, all these properties are well-known propositions about the classical definition.

Following
[
RS87
]
we define the
\mathrm{UCT}
-class in
\mathrm{KK}
as the localizing subcategory generated by the tensor unit
\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})
.
Using
1.4
.
2
we will see in
9.16
that the natural map
\mathrm{KK}(B,-)\to\mathrm{E}(B,-)
is an equivalence if
B
belongs to the
\mathrm{UCT}
-class. Essentially by definition, the
K
-theory functor
induces a symmetric monoidal equivalence between the
\mathrm{UCT}
-class and the stable
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{Mod}}(\mathrm{KU})
. This leads to a simple picture of the universal coefficient theorem and the Künneth formula also formulated in
9.16
.

The Cuntz picture
[
Cun87
]
of
KK
-theory is based on the
q
-construction which involves a functor and a natural transformation

q:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad\iota:q\to{\mathtt{id}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}\ .

The goal of
Section

11
is to study
the homotopical features of the
q
-construction. This whole section is essentially a translation of
[
Cun87
]
from abelian group valued functors to functors having values in semi-additive or additive
\infty
-categories. The main insight derived in this section is that inverting the image of the set

\{\iota_{A}:qA\to A\mid A\in C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\}
in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
(see Step
2
above) yields the universal homotopy invariant, stable split- and Schochet exact functor

L_{h,K,q}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h,q}

with values in a left-exact additive
\infty
-category.
By
11.6
it is a Dwyer-Kan localization.
Using the deep result
[
Cun87
, Thm 1.6]
(reproduced in these notes as
Theorem

11.13
) we will see in the separable case that the Dwyer-Kan localization
L_{\mathrm{sep},q}:L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}\to L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
is actually a right Bousfield localization, and we obtain the very simple formula

\ell\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(qA,K\otimes B)\simeq{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}(A,B)

(1.2)

for the mapping space between two
separable

C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
in this localization. Here the left-hand side of this equivalence is the space associated to the topological space of homomorphisms from
qA
to
K\otimes B
.

By
[
Cun87
]
it is known that for two separable
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B

there is an isomorphism

\pi_{0}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(qA,K\otimes B)\cong\mathrm{KK}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}(A,B)\ .

(1.3)

It is probably the deepest challenge of these notes to provide an accessible proof of the analogue

\pi_{0}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(qA,K\otimes B)\cong\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(A,B)

(1.4)

of this formula with classical
KK
-theory replaced by the the homotopy theoretic version constructed in the present paper.
Note that in contrast to
[
Cun87
]
, where
(
1.3
) is essentially the definition of the right-hand side,
in our situation the group on the right of (
1.4
) is defined as the group of components of the mapping space
in a certain Dwyer-Kan localization.
By (
1.2
) and the possibility to replace
B
by suspensions
S^{i}(B)
for
i
in
{\rm Nature}
we see that (
1.4
)
is equivalent to the fact that the functor
L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
is equivalent to the functor
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
. An equivalent formulation of this latter fact is
the automatic semiexactness
Theorem

12.4
stating that for any additive left-exact
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
the natural inclusion

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,se+Sch}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,splt+Sch}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})

from homotopy invariant, stable and Schochet- and semiexact functors to homotopy invariant, stable and Schochet- and split-exact functors is an equivalence. The proof of the automatic semiexactness theorem
will be discussed in detail in
Section

12
.

Note that semiexactness of an exact sequence of
C^{*}
-algebras is defined in terms of the existence of a completely positive contractive (cpc) split. Since this is an analytic condition which somehow has to be exploited it is not surprising that
the proof of the automatic semiexactness theorem in
Section

12
is not purely homotopy theoretic in nature but contains various analytic arguments. But since we will avoid to use Kasparov products or other deep results from the classical theory it might be quite accessible to homotopy theorists. In particular note that our proof does not depend on the formula (
1.2
), whose proof in
Theorem

11.13
involves
Pedersen’s derivation lifting.

But using (
1.2
) and the automatic semiexactness theorem together in
12.3
we can show
also in the context of the present notes
that
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
admits countable colimits and is therefore idempotent complete. In
[
BEL
, Lem. 2.19]

this fact has been shown by importing
[
Kas88
, Thm. 2.9]
.

1
1

1

Using the results from
[
BD24
]
one can show that also

\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}
admits countable coproducts.

The classical construction of
KK
-theory is based on the notion of Kasparov modules. Equivalence classes of Kasparov modules are interpreted as elements of

\mathrm{KK}_{0}(A,B)
. Kasparov modules in a certain standard form can be captured by the Cuntz picture in terms of the
q
-construction.
Essentially by definition, the left-hand side of (
1.2
) can be interpreted
as the space of Kasparov
(A,B)
-modules.
So the mapping spaces in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
are expressed in terms of spaces of Kasparov modules via (
1.2
), while the
automatic semiexactness theorem
implies that these are also the mapping spaces in
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
In the present note we will not discuss the alternative models for the group-valued
KK
-theory based on asymptotic morphisms

[
Tho99
]
or localization algebras
[
DWW18
]
.

Recall that the classical concrete model of
E
-theory
[
CH90
]
involves asymptotic morphisms.
In
Section

14
we will show
that asymptotic morphisms give rise to elements in
\mathrm{E}_{0}(A,B)

in a way which is compatible with compositions.

We finally stress that these notes concentrate on the homotopy theoretic and categorical aspects of
KK
- and
E
-theory.
The full power of
KK
-theory to applications e.g. to the classification programmes for
C^{*}
-algebras
only unrolls itself if one employs the equivalence of different cycle-by-relation models based on Kasparov modules.
This aspect will not be considered at all in these notes.
Other applications e.g. to the Baum-Connes or Novikov conjecture require the ability to control the composition of morphisms in
KK
-theory explicitly.
If one uses the model based on Kasparov modules, then there are well-developed methods serving
this purpose e.g. using connections. In the model given in the present paper it is quite tricky to calculate
compositions of morphisms which do not simply come from morphisms between the
C^{*}
-algebras. We actually do only one non-trivial example of such a composition which is
12.12
which is already complicated enough. But note that this calculation is absolutely crucial since it provides the last cornerstone for the automatic semiexactness theorem which also goes into the comparison result
1.3
.

These notes present an expanded version of the lecture notes of a course on
Noncommutative Homotopy Theory

tought at the University of Regensburg in the winter term 2022/23. The author thanks the participants for suspicious attention and various critical remarks.

Acknowledgement: The author was supported by the SFB 1085 (Higher Invariants) funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG). The author profited from discussions with M. Land und U. Pennig which started this project. Special thanks go to B. Dünzinger for carefully reading the draft.

2
C^{*}
-algebras

In this section we collect the basic facts from
C^{*}
-algebra theory which we will use in the present paper.
The material can be found in the introductory chapters of textbooks like
[
Dix77
]
,
[
Ped79
]
,
[
BO08
]
,
[
Wil07
]
,
[
Pis
]
and many others.

In order to fix set-theoretic size issues we choose three Grothendieck universes called the small, large, and very large sets.

In
2.4
we will introduce the notions appearing in
1.1
and
1.2
.

We let
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
denote the large, but locally small category of small
C^{*}
-algebras and homomorphisms. By
C^{\ast}\mathbf{Alg}
we denote its subcategory of unital
C^{*}
-algebras and unit-preserving homomorphisms.
As we are interested in the categorical properties of the categories of
C^{*}
-algebras we will follow the approach in
[
Bun
]
.
We consider
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
as a full subcategory of the large locally small category of small
*
-algebras
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
. The latter is the category of small (possibly non-unital) algebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
with an antilinear involution
*
and structure-preserving maps.

A
C^{*}
-seminorm
on a
*
-algebra
A
is a submultiplicative seminorm satisfying the
C^{*}
-equality
\|a^{*}a\|=\|a\|^{2}
. For
a
in
A
we define the
maximal seminorm
of
a
by
\|a\|_{\max}:=\sup_{\|-\|}\|a\|
, where the supremum runs over all
C^{*}
-seminorms on
A
.

We say that
A
is a
pre-
C^{*}
-algebra
if all its elements have a finite maximal seminorm. The inclusion
{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{pre}}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
of the category of pre-
C^{*}
-algebras into the category of all
*
-algebras
is the left-adjoint of a right Bousfield localization whose right-adjoint is the
bounded elements functor

\mathrm{Bd}^{\infty}
.

A
C^{*}
-algebra
is a pre-
C^{*}
-algebra
A
with the property that
(A,\|-\|_{\max})
is a Banach space.
The inclusion
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{pre}}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of the category of
C^{*}
-algebras into the category of pre-
C^{*}
-algebras is the right-adjoint of a left Bousfield localization whose left adjoint is the
completion functor

\mathrm{compl}
.

In view of its algebraic description the category
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
is clearly complete and cocomplete in the sense that it admits all small limits and colimit.
As a consequence of the above characterization of
C^{*}
-algebras
the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is complete and cocomplete, too. We furthermore obtain an explicit description of limits and colimits in terms of their algebraic counterparts indicated by a superscript
\mathrm{alg}
. If
A:I\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is an
I
-diagram of
C^{*}
-algebras for some small index category
I
, then

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{I}A\cong\mathrm{Bd}^{\infty}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{I}}^{\mathrm{alg}}A)\ ,\quad\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I}A\cong\mathrm{compl}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I}}^{\mathrm{alg}}A)\ .

(2.1)

In particular, the coproduct of the
C^{*}
-algebras
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
is represented by the free product of
C^{*}
-algebras
A_{0}*A_{1}:=\mathrm{compl}(A_{0}*^{\mathrm{alg}}A_{1})

together with the canonical morphisms
\iota_{i}:A_{i}\to A_{0}*A_{1}
. Similarly, the product of
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
is represented by the (algebraic) sum
A_{0}\oplus A_{1}
together with the canonical projections
{\mathtt{pr}}_{i}:A_{0}\oplus A_{1}\to A_{i}
.
If
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
is a small infinite family of
C^{*}
-algebras, then (
2.1
) says that

\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}\cong\mathrm{Bd}^{\infty}(\prod_{i\in I}^{\mathrm{alg}}A_{i})
is the subalgebra of the algebraic product of families
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of elements
a_{i}
in
A_{i}
with
\sup_{i\in I}\|a_{i}\|_{A_{i}}<\infty
.

From now on we will suppress the size adjectives large and small as much as possible.

The category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is pointed by the zero algebra
0
.

The category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
has two canonical symmetric monoidal structures
\otimes_{\max}
and
\otimes_{\min}
.
For
C^{*}
-algebras
A,B
the
maximal tensor product

is defined by
A\otimes_{\max}B:=\mathrm{compl}(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B)
,
where we use that the
*
-algebra
A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B
is actually a pre-
C^{*}
-algebra.

In order to define the
minimal tensor product
(also called the spatial tensor product) we equip
A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B
with the minimal
C^{*}
-norm (not seminorm!) and form the closure.
This minimal norm can alternatively be characterized as the norm induced by the representation
A\otimes B\to B(H\otimes L)
induced by any two faithful representations
A\to B(L)
and
B\to B(H)
for Hilbert spaces
L
and
H
.

We always have a canonical morphism
A\otimes_{\max}B\to A\otimes_{\min}B
, and
A
is called
nuclear
if this morphism is an isomorphism for all
B
. Its is known that commutative
C^{*}
-algebras and the
C^{*}
-algebra
K
of compact operators on a separable Hilbert space are nuclear.
If one of the tensor factors is nuclear we can safely write
\otimes
and omit the subscript specifying the choice.

Example 2.1
.

The commutative algebra objects
\mathbf{CAlg}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
(say with respect to
\otimes_{\max}
)
are precisely the unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebras.
∎

If
X
is a compact topological space, then by
C(X)
we denote the commutative
C^{*}
-algebra of continuous
{\mathbb{C}}
-valued functions on
X
. For
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
we let
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B)
denote the compactly generated topological space characterized by the property that for every
compact
space
X
we have a natural bijection

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B))\cong{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,C(X)\otimes B)\ .

(2.2)

The topology on
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B)

is equivalent to the maximal compactly generated topology containing the point-norm topology on

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,B)
. In this way
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
becomes a
category enriched in topological spaces
.

A homomorphism
f:B\to C
between
C^{*}
-algebras is a
homotopy equivalence
if there exists a homomorphism
g:C\to B
, called a
homotopy inverse
,
such that
f\circ g
is homotopic to
{\mathtt{id}}_{C}
in
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(C,C)
and

g\circ f
is homotopic to
{\mathtt{id}}_{B}
in
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(B,B)
. Equivalently, one could require that the
induced map
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,f):\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B)\to\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,C)
is a homotopy equivalence of topological spaces for all
C^{*}
-algebras
A
.

A
left upper corner inclusion

A\to A\otimes K
is a homomorphism of the form
a\mapsto a\otimes e
where
e
is a minimal non-zero projection in
K
.

Remark 2.2
.

If one interprets
K
and
A\otimes K
as algebras of
{\rm Nature}
-indexed matrices with entries in
{\mathbb{C}}
or
A
, respectively, then we can write this map as

a\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}a&0&0&\dots\\
0&0&0&\dots\\
0&0&0&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{array}\right)\ .

This picture explains the name
left upper corner
inclusion.
∎

An exact sequence

0\to I\to B\xrightarrow{\pi}Q\to 0

of
C^{*}
-algebras is called
semisplit exact
(or
split exact
), if
\pi
admits a completely positive contractive (cpc) right-inverse (or a right-inverse homomorphism, respectively).
It is known that the functor
A\otimes_{\max}-
preserves exact sequences and in addition the condition
of being semi-split exact or split-exact. The functor
A\otimes_{\min}-
preserves semi-split exact sequences and split-exact sequences. A cartesian square in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

is called

exact (semisplit)
if the vertical maps are surjective (admit a cpc split). The functor
A\otimes_{\max}-
preserves exact cartesian squares and also semisplit cartesian squares, and
A\otimes_{\min}-
preserves semisplit ones. Note that the fact that
B\to C
is surjective or admits a cpc split implies that
E\to D
has the same property.

A
C^{*}
-algebra is called
separable
if it contains a countable dense subset.
We let
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
denote the full subcategory of separable
C^{*}
-algebras.
Note that
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is essentially small.
For a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
we let
A^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}A
denote the poset of separable subalgebras of
A
.
Then we have a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{A^{\prime}\subseteq A}A^{\prime}\cong A.

(2.3)

Example 2.3
.

The algebra of compact operators
K(H)
on a separable Hilbert space
H
is separable.
If
\dim(H)=\infty
, then the algebra of bounded operators
B(H)
is not separable.
If
X
is a separable metric space, then
C_{0}(X)
is a separable
C^{*}
-algebra.
If
X
is not compact, then the
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded continuous functions
C_{b}(X)
on
X
is not separable.

∎

Let
F
be a functor defined on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
or
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.

Definition 2.4
.

1.

F
is homotopy invariant if
F
sends homotopy equivalences to equivalences.

2.

F
is stable if it sends left-upper corner inclusions to equivalences.

3.

F
is reduced if
F(0)
is a zero object.

4.

F
is exact (semi-split exact, split-exact) if
F
is reduced and
F
sends exact (semi-split exact or split-exact) sequences to fibre sequences.

5.

If
F
is defined on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then we say that
F
is
s
-finitary if for every
C^{*}
-algebra
A
the canonical morphism

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{A^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}A}F(A^{\prime})\to F(A)
is an equivalence.

Here in
2.4
.
3
and
2.4
.
4

we implicitly assume that the target of
F
is pointed. In

2.4
.
5
we further assume that the colimit exists.

Remark 2.5
.

In order to check that
F
is homotopy invariant it suffices to check that
F(A)\to F(C([0,1])\otimes A)
is an equivalence for every
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, where the map is induced by
({\mathbb{C}}\to C([0,1]))\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{A}
.

The functor
F
is
s
-finitary if and only if it represents the left Kan-exaction of the restriction
F_{|C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}

along the inclusion
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
In view of (
2.3
) a filtered colimit preserving functor is
s
-finitary.

∎

Remark 2.6
.

Many constructions of the present paper done for
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
have a version for separable algebras.
We will indicate this in the notation by adding subscripts
\mathrm{sep}
to the categories or functors.
If everything goes through for separable algebras word by word, then we will simply state that we
have a separable version.
At some places separability matters, and then we will be explicit.
∎

3
Inverting homotopy equivalences

In this section we study the Dwyer-Kan localization of the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
at the set of homotopy equivalences.
We will show that the resulting
\infty
-category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is presented by the topological enriched version of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
so that we understand the mapping spaces in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
explicitly. It will turn out that
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}

is a pointed left-exact
\infty
-category.

We start with recalling the
\infty
-categorical background on Dwyer-Kan localizations.
Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a
\infty
-category and
W
be a set of morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}
. Then we can form the
Dywer-Kan localization

L:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}[W^{-1}]

of
{\mathbf{C}}
at
W
. It is characterized by the universal property
that

L^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathbf{C}}[W^{-1}],{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{W}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}})

(3.1)

is an equivalence for every
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
, where the superscript
W
on the right indicates the full subcategory of functors which send the elements of
W
to equivalences
[
Lur
, Def. 1.3.4.1 & Rem. 1.3.4.2]
. In the present paper we will apply this to
\infty
-categories
{\mathbf{C}}
,
{\mathbf{D}}
in the universe of large sets
so that
{\mathbf{C}}[W^{-1}]
also belongs to this universe.

Remark 3.1
.

The functor
L
is essentially surjective and in order to make formulas more readable we will usually denote the image
L(C)
or
L(f)
in
{\mathbf{C}}[W^{-1}]
of an object or morphism in
{\mathbf{C}}

simply by
C
or
f
. This convention in particular applies when we insert them into functors defined on
{\mathbf{C}}[W^{-1}]
. But sometimes we need the longer, more precise notation in order to avoid confusion.

∎

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is symmetric monoidal, then we say that the localization
L
admits a symmetric monoidal refinement, if

{\mathbf{C}}[W^{-1}]
has a symmetric monoidal structure,
L
has a symmetric monoial refinement, and we have an equivalence

L^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}_{\otimes/\mathrm{lax}}({\mathbf{C}}[W^{-1}],{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{W}_{\otimes/\mathrm{lax}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}})

(3.2)

for every symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
, where
the notation
\otimes/\mathrm{lax}
indicates two separate formulas, one for symmetric monoidal functors and one for lax symmetric monoidal functors.

In order to check that
L
has a symmetric monoidal refinement by
[
Hin16
, Def.3.3.2]
it suffices to check that the functor
C\otimes-
preserves
W
for every object
C
of
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Definition 3.2
.

We let

L_{h}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}

(3.3)

be the Dwyer-Kan localization of the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
at the homotopy equivalences.

By definition it is characterized by the universal property that
pull-back along
L_{h}
induces for any
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
an equivalence

L_{h}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})\ ,

(3.4)

where the superscript
h
indicates the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Fun}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})
of homotopy invariant functors (see
2.4
.
1
).

We consider the tensor product
\otimes_{?}
on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
for
?
in
\{\max,\min\}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
?
in
\{\max,\min\}

the localization
L_{h}
has a symmetric monoidal refinement.

Proof.

It follows from the functoriality and associativity of
\otimes_{?}
and (
2.2
) that for
every
C^{*}
-algebra
A
the functor
A\otimes_{?}-:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is continuous for the topological enrichment and therefore preserves
homotopy equivalences. This implies that
L_{h}
has a symmetric monoidal refinement. ∎

Thus for every
symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
we have an equivalence

L_{h}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}_{\otimes/\mathrm{lax}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h}_{\otimes/\mathrm{lax}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})\ .

(3.5)

Remark 3.4
.

Note that on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
we have two symmetric monoidal structures
\otimes_{?}
, one for
?=\max
and one for
?=\min
which will be discussed in a parallel manner. In particular, (
3.5
) actually has two versions.
∎

In contrast to general Dwyer Kan localizations, in the present case we can understand the mapping spaces in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
explicitly. In fact, we will see that the topologically enriched category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
directly presents the localization. To this end we
apply the singular complex functor
{\mathtt{sing}}
to the
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}
-spaces in order to get a Kan-complex enriched category. Further applying
the homotopy coherent nerve we get an
\infty
-category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\infty}
together with a functor

C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\infty}
given by the inclusion of the zero skeleton of the mapping spaces.

Proposition 3.5
.

The functor
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\infty}
presents the
Dwyer-Kan localization of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
at the homotopy equivalences.

Proof.

For every
C^{*}
-algebra
B
we define the
path algebra

PB:=C(\Delta^{1})\otimes B\ .

(3.6)

By (
2.2
), defining a map of simplicial sets
[n]\to{\mathtt{sign}}(\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B))
is equivalent to specifying an element in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,C(\Delta^{n})\otimes B)
. We let

h_{B}:[1]\to{\mathtt{sign}}(\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(PB,B))
correspond to the identity of
PB
. One then checks that for any
C^{*}
-algebra
A
the canonically induced map

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{sSet}}}([n],{\mathtt{sing}}(\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,PB)))\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{sSet}}}([1]\times[n],{\mathtt{sing}}(\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B)))

is a bijection. The assertion of
3.5
now follows from
[
Lur
, Thm 1.3.4.7]
.
∎

Corollary 3.6
.

The
\infty
-category

C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is locally small.

Remark 3.7
.

At various places in this note we will use that small topological spaces present objects in the large
\infty
-category of small spaces
2
2

2

This name is changed to
anima
in recent literature.

\mathbf{Spc}
. This is achieved by the functor

\ell:{\mathbf{Top}}\to\mathbf{Spc}

(3.7)

which presents the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Spc}
as the Dwyer-Kan localization of
{\mathbf{Top}}

at the set of weak homotopy equivalences. It is one of the fundamental principles called the
Grothendieck’s homotopy
hypothesis
that the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Spc}
defined in this way is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-groupoids in which the mapping spaces of locally small
\infty
-categories naturally live.
For a general large
\infty
-category they belong to the very large
\infty
-category of large spaces which we will denote by
\mathbf{SPC}
.

We will use that
\ell
preserves coproducts, products and sends Serre fibrant cartesian squares to cartesian squares,
were a cartesian square

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U}

in
{\mathbf{Top}}
is called
Serre fibrant
if
f
is a Serre fibration.

∎

As an immediate corollary of Proposition
3.5
we get an explicit description of the mapping spaces in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
.

Corollary 3.8
.

For any two
C^{*}
-algebras
A,B
we have a natural equivalence of spaces

{\mathtt{Map}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}}(A,B)\simeq\ell\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B)\ .

(3.8)

In the formula above we adopted the conventions from
3.1
.

We now discuss limits and colimits in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

The category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
admits finite products and arbitrary small coproducts, and the localization
L_{h}
preserves them.

Proof.

We start with finite products.
Let
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
be a finite family of
C^{*}
-algebras and
A
be any
C^{*}
-algebra. Then we must show that the
map

{\mathtt{Map}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}}(A,\prod_{i}B_{i})\to\prod_{i\in I}{\mathtt{Map}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,B_{i})

induced by the family of projections

(\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}\to B_{j})_{j\in I}

is an equivalence.
But this follows from the fact that

\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,\prod_{i}B_{i})\to\prod_{i\in I}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B_{i})

(3.9)

is actually a homeomorphism.

We now consider coproducts. Let
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
be a small family of
C^{*}
-algebras and
B
be any
C^{*}
-algebra. Then we must show that the map

{\mathtt{Map}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}}(\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i},B)\to\prod_{i\in I}{\mathtt{Map}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A_{i},B)

induced by the family of inclusions
(A_{j}\to\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i})_{j\in I}
is an equivalence.
Again this follows from the fact that

\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i},B)\to\prod_{i\in I}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A_{i},B)

is actually a homeomorphism.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

In the case of products we assume that the index set
I
is finite. If it is not finite, then the map
(
3.9
) is no longer a homeomorphism.
Let
X
be a compact topological space.
Then the image under (
3.9
) of
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,\prod_{i}B_{i}))
in

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\prod_{i\in I}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B_{i}))\cong\prod_{i\in I}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(X,\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B_{i}))

consists of the families of maps

(\phi_{i}:X\to\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B_{i}))_{i\in I}
such that
the family
(\phi_{i}(a):X\to B_{i})_{i\in I}
is equicontinuous for every
a
in
A
.

∎

Lemma 3.11
.

The functor
L_{h}
is reduced and

C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is pointed.

Proof.

The zero algebra represents the inital and the final object of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
.
∎

Example 3.12
.

Let
A
be any
C^{*}
-algebra. Then
C_{0}([0,\infty))\otimes A
also represents the zero object in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
.

∎

A morphism
f:B\to C
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is called a
Schochet fibration
if the map

f_{*}:\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,B)\to\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,C)
is a Serre fibration of topological spaces for every
C^{*}
-algebra
A

[
Sch84
]
.

Example 3.13
.

If
i:Y\to X
is a map of compact spaces which has the homotopy extension property, then the restriction map
i^{*}:C(X)\to C(Y)
is a Schochet fibration
which in addition admits a cpc split.

∎

A cartesian square

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

is called
Schochet fibrant
if
f
is a Schochet fibration.
Note that a Schochet fibration is automatically surjective.
If
D=0
, then we say that

0\to A\to B\to C\to 0

is a
Schochet exact
sequence.

Definition 3.14
.

A functor
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
will be called Schochet exact
if it sends Schochet fibrant cartesian squares to cartesian squares.

We will indicate Schochet exact functors by a super script as in
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{Sch}
.

Remark 3.15
.

In contrast to the other notions of exactness introduced in
2.4
.
4
, the notion of Schochet exactness is formulated in terms of squares instead of exact sequences.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is pointed, then a reduced Schochet exact functor sends Schochet exact sequences to fibre sequences.
If
{\mathbf{C}}
is stable, then it is easy to see that the converse is also true. A functor which sends Schochet exact sequences
to fibre sequences is reduced and Schochet exact, see
[
BEL
, Lem. 2.14]
for analogous statements for semiexact functors and squares.

∎

Remark 3.16
.

For the proof of
3.17
.
5
below we need the mapping cylinder construction.
The
mapping cylinder
of a map
f:B\to C
of
C^{*}
-algebras is defined by the Schochet fibrant and semisplit cartesian square

(3.10)

where

is the path algebra as in (
3.6
).
The maps

h_{f}:Z(f)\to B
and
{\mathtt{ev}}_{0}
are homotopy equivalences.
We write elements in
Z(f)
as pairs
(b,\gamma)
with
b
in
B
and
\gamma
in
PC
such that
\gamma(0)=f(b)
. The map

\tilde{f}:Z(f)\to C
given by
(b,\gamma)\mapsto\gamma(1)
is a Schochet fibration and also admits a cpc split
c\mapsto(0,\gamma_{c})
with
\gamma_{c}(t):=tc
.
We further define the
mapping cone
of
f
by
C(f):={\mathtt{ker}}(\tilde{f})
.
An element of
C(f)
is thus a pair
(a,\gamma)
of an element of
A
and a path in
C
with
f(a)=\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(1)=0
.

The sequence

0\to C(f)\xrightarrow{\iota_{f}}Z(f)\xrightarrow{\tilde{f}}C\to 0

(3.11)

is Schochet- and semi-split exact.

∎

Recall that an
\infty
-category is called
left-exact
if it admits all finite limits. A functor between left-exact
\infty
-categories is called
left-exact
if it preserves finite limits. We use the notation
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{lex}}
in order to denote the full subcategory of left-exact functors.

Proposition 3.17
.

1.

The
\infty
-category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is left-exact.

2.

The functor
L_{h}
sends Schochet fibrant cartesian squares to cartesian squares.

3.

The pull-back along
L_{h}
induces for every left-exact
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}

an equivalence

L_{h}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{lex}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,Sch}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})\ .

(3.12)

4.

The pull-back along the symmetric monoidal refinement of
L_{h}
induces for every symmetric monoidal left-exact
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}

an equivalence

L_{h}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{lex}}_{\otimes,\mathrm{lax}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,Sch}_{\otimes,\mathrm{lax}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})\ .

5.

For
?
in
\{\min,\max\}

the functor
-\otimes_{?}-:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}\times C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is bi left-exact.

Proof.

We let
W
be the subcategory of homotopy equivalences in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
F
be the subcategory of Schochet fibrations. Then

(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},W,F)
is a category of fibrant objects in the sense of
[
Cis19
, Def. 7.4.12 and Def. 7.5.7]
.
The corresponding verifications are due to
[
Uuy13
, Thm. 2.19]
.
The main point is to see that the pull-back of a Schochet fibration or of a homotopy equivalence is again a Schochet fibration or a homotopy equivalence.

The Assertions
1
and
2
then follow from
[
Cis19
, Prop. 7.5.6]
.
For
2
, in view of
3.8
one could argue alternatively that
\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(A,-)
sends
Schochet fibrant cartesian squares to Serre fibrant cartesian squares, and that
\ell
sends Serre fibrant cartesian squares
to cartesian squares.

We now show Assertion
3
.
By Assertion
2
it is clear that
L_{h}^{*}
in (
3.12
) sends left-exact functors to Schochet exact functors. Since it is the restriction of the equivalence in (
3.4
) it is fully faithful.
We argue that it also essentially surjective. Let
F
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,Sch}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})
. Then by (
3.4
) there exists
\hat{F}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h},{\mathbf{D}})
such that
L_{h}^{*}\hat{F}\simeq F
.
We must show that
\hat{F}
is left-exact. Since it is reduced it suffices to show that it preserves cartesian squares.

In view of
3.8

any diagram of the shape

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}

is equivalent to the image under
L_{h}
of a diagram of this shape in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We can replace
f
by

\tilde{f}:Z(f)\to C
without changing the image of the diagram under
L_{h}
up to equivalence.
We now complete the diagram to a cartesian square

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

(3.13)

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
It is Schochet fibrant and semisplit. Its image under
L_{h}
is then a cartesian square in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
, and every cartesian square in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is equivalent to one of this form. The image under
\hat{F}
of
L_{h}
of the latter square is equivalent to the image under
F
of the original Schochet fibrant cartesian square and hence a cartesian square.

Assertion
4
follows from a combination of Assertion
3
and the equivalence (
3.5
).

In the following
\otimes
stands for
\otimes_{\max}
or
\otimes_{\min}
.
Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. It follows from
3.9
that the endofunctor
A\otimes-:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is reduced and preserves finite products. So it suffices to show that it preserves cartesian squares.
As seen above every cartesian square in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is equivalent to the image under
L_{h}
of a square of the form (
3.13
).

Using the exactness of
A\otimes_{\max}-
or the semi-exactness of
A\otimes_{\min}-
we see that

\textstyle{A\otimes P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\otimes Z(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{id}}_{A}\otimes\tilde{f}}
\textstyle{A\otimes D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\otimes C}

(3.14)

is again cartesian. By analysing the application of
A\otimes-
to (
3.10
) we can obtain an
isomorphism

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.89583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.89583pt\raise We conclude
that the square (
3.14
) is again a Schochet fibrant cartesian square, and that its image under
L_{h}
is a cartesian square in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
.
∎

Example 3.18
.

The functor
L_{h}
sends the sequence (
3.11
) to a fibre sequence. Since the square

\textstyle{Z(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{h_{f}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C}

commutes up to a preferred homotopy it provides an equivalence between
L_{h}(\tilde{f})
and
L_{h}(f)
.
In particular, the mapping cone
C(f)
represents the fibre of
L_{h}(f)
.
∎

Example 3.19
.

A pointed left-exact
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
has a loop endofunctor

\Omega:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
For an object
C
of
{\mathbf{C}}
the object
\Omega C
is determined by
the pull-back

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.54236pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.54236pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.9736pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.38887pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 35.9736pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.4736pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.54236pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.54236pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(3.15)

The category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
has the suspension endofunctor

S:=C_{0}({\mathbb{R}})\otimes-:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

The square of restriction maps

\textstyle{C_{0}({\mathbb{R}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C_{0}((-\infty,0])}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{ev}}_{0}}
\textstyle{C_{0}([0,\infty))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{ev}}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}}

is a Schochet fibrant semisplit cartesian square by
3.13
.
Applying
L_{h}(-\otimes A)
we get a cartesian square whose lower left and upper right corners are zero objects by
3.12
. We therefore obtain an equivalence of endofunctors

L_{h}\circ S\simeq\Omega\circ L_{h}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}\ .

(3.16)

∎

Example 3.20
.

In this example we consider the Puppe sequence associated to a morphism
f:A\to B
.
The latter gives rise to the mapping cone sequence (
3.11
).
Since
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
is left-exact we can form the following diagram of pull-back squares

\textstyle{\Omega^{2}L_{h}(B))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega L_{h}(\partial_{f})}
\textstyle{\Omega L_{h}(C(f))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega L_{h}(i_{f})}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega L_{h}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega L_{h}(f)}
\textstyle{\Omega L_{h}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{f}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{h}(C(f))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{L_{h}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{L_{h}(B)}

in

C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
. The lower square is cartesian by
3.18
.
We further use the homotopy invariance of
L_{h}
applied to the homotopy equivalence
h_{f}
in order to replace
L_{h}(Z(f))
by
L_{h}(A)
, and
we use (
3.15
) in order to identify the corners with iterated loops of the objects. E.g. for the corner

\Omega L_{h}(A)
just observe that the horizontal composition of the two middle squares is again cartesian.
By (
3.16
) we can express looping in terms of suspension. The sequence

\dots\to L_{h}(S^{2}(B))\to L_{h}(S(C(f)))\to L_{h}(S(A))\to L_{h}(S(B))\to L_{h}(C(f))\to L_{h}(A)\to L_{h}(B)

(3.17)

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}

is called the
Puppe sequence
associated to
f
. Each consecutive pair of morphisms is a part of a fibre sequence in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
.

∎

.

{\mathbf{C}}
is some

-category with a set of morphisms

where the vertical functors are Dwyer Kan localizations. In general the lower horizontal map is not fully faithful.
But this is true if

,

are the homotopy equivalences, and

.
∎

We
write
L_{\mathrm{sep},h}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
for the corresponding Dwyer-Kan localization.
Thus for any
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
pull-back along
L_{\mathrm{sep},h}
induces an equivalence

L_{\mathrm{sep},h}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})\ .

Note that
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
is essentially small.
Using

3.8
,
3.17
and its separable version and the fact that the tensor products preserve separable algebras we get the following statements.

Corollary 3.22
.

1.

We have a commutative square

whose vertical arrows are Dwyer-Kan localizations and whose horizontal arrows are fully faithful.

The square in

. has a refinement to a diagram of symmetric monoidal categories and symmetric monoidal functors for

with

such that

(3.18)

the functor
