148.20195pt\raise-96.29333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 148.20195pt\raise-96.29333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(qA)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 165.01988pt\raise-34.47516pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.39175pt\raise 0.92007pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.39175pt\raise-0.92007pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.39175pt\raise 0.92007pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.39175pt\raise-0.92007pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.39175pt\raise 0.92007pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.39175pt\raise-0.92007pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 241.7011pt\raise-96.29333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 241.7011pt\raise-96.29333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 298.41077pt\raise-96.29333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

All vertical maps are injective. The
C^{*}
-algebra

R
is defined as the subalgebra of
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(QqA)
generated by
matrices of the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}(qA)&\eta_{0}(qA)\eta_{1}(qA)\\
\eta_{1}(qA)\eta_{0}(qA)&\eta_{1}(qA)\end{array}\right)\ ,

where we use the notation
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
for the canonical inclusions
\iota_{0}
and
\iota_{1}

of
qA
into
QqA
.
We observe that the projection
R\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(qA)
is surjective and defines the ideal
J
as its kernel.
We let
D
be the subalgebra of
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(Q^{2}A)
generated by the image of
R
and the elements

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}(\iota_{0}(a))&0\\
0&\eta_{1}(\iota_{0}(a))\end{array}\right)\ ,\quad a\in A\ .

One checks by an explicit calculation that
R
is an ideal in
D
, and hence
J
is also an ideal in
D
.
Then
S
is the subalgebra of
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(QA)
generated by
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(qA)
and the diagonal elements

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\iota_{0}(a)&0\\
0&\iota_{0}(a)\end{array}\right)\ ,\quad a\in A\ .

We let
U_{t}
be the rotation matrix from (
4.7
). We note that
conjugation by
U_{t}
on
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(QA)
preserves the subalgebra
S
. The derivative
of this action is a bounded derivation
\bar{\delta}
of
S
.

By Pedersen’s derivation lifting theorem
[
Ped76
]
,
[
Ped79
, Thm 8.6.15]
there exists a
derivation
\delta
of
D
such that
\pi\circ\delta=\bar{\delta}\circ\pi
. It is at this point
where separability of
A
is important. There are counterexamples
to the derivation lifting theorem for non-separable algebras.

We define the family
\sigma_{t}:=e^{t\delta}
of automorphisms of
D
and set
\sigma:=\sigma_{1}
.

We now define
\phi:qA\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(q^{2}A)
as the homomorphism with the components

\hat{\phi}_{i}:A\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(QqA)\to M({\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(q^{2}A))
given by

\hat{\phi}_{0}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{0}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}\end{array}\right)\ ,\quad\hat{\phi}_{1}:=\sigma\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{1}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}\end{array}\right)\ .

(11.28)

In order to see that the application of
\sigma
is well-defined we
rewrite

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{1}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{0}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ(\iota_{1}-\iota_{0})&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right)

which obviously takes values in
D
. Using in addition a similar rewriting of
\hat{\phi}_{0}

one then
checks that
\hat{\phi}_{0}-\hat{\phi}_{1}
takes values in
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(q^{2}A)
.

The homotopy
\gamma_{t}
from the left upper corner inclusion
q^{2}A\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(q^{2}A)
to
\phi\circ\iota_{qA}
has the components
\hat{\gamma}_{i}:qA\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(Q^{2}A)
given by the map (again given by a pair of components)

\hat{\gamma}_{0}:=\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{0}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}\end{array}\right)\ ,\sigma_{t}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{1}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{1}\end{array}\right)\right)

and the map

\hat{\gamma}_{1}:=\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{1}\end{array}\right)\ ,U_{t}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{1}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}\end{array}\right)U_{t}^{*}\right)\ .

Similarly, a homotopy
\lambda_{t}
from the left upper corner inclusion

qA\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(qA)
to
\iota_{qA}\circ\phi
has the components
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(p_{0})\circ\tilde{\lambda}_{t}:qA\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(qA)
,
where
p_{0}:QqA\to qA
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{t}:qA\to R\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(QqA)
is given by

\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{0}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{1}\end{array}\right)\ ,\sigma_{t}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\eta_{0}\circ\iota_{1}&0\\
0&\eta_{1}\circ\iota_{0}\end{array}\right)\right)\ .

We leave the justifications for these formulas to the interested reader or refer to the proof of
[
Cun87
, Thm. 1.6]
.
∎

12
The automatic semiexactness theorem

Since the symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is homotopy invariant, stable and split-exact, it
belongs to the right-hand side of the separable version of the equivalence (
11.23
) describing the universal property of
L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}
for
{\mathbf{D}}:=\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
Its preimage under this equivalence
is the left-exact and symmetric monoidal functor
h
depicted by the lower horizontal arrow in the commutative triangle

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 37.69003pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 61.69003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.2541pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.5131pt\raise-9.30556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 135.00214pt\raise-24.11111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 147.02682pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-37.69003pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.67255pt\raise-26.01387pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{h}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.42886pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 82.55945pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 133.42886pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(12.1)

The functor
h
will be called the
comparison functor
.

In
Theorem

12.1
we claim that this comparison functor is an equivalence. We will give two immediate proofs
which at least implicitly assume the formulas (
11.1
), (
11.2
) and
Theorem

13.16
. They therefore involve more than just the simple homotopy theoretic considerations from the present notes. In order to provide a selfcontained proof we will formulate two equivalent statements
Theorem

12.4
and
Theorem

12.5
.
Note that
Theorem

12.4
is just an assertion about functors defined on the category of separable
C^{*}
-algebras and does not require any
K
-theoretic element at all. On the other hand, the argument for

Theorem

12.5
is quite accessible to the methods developed here so that we
write out the details of the argument for this version. This finally also verifies (
11.2
) in a non-circular manner.

The fact that the comparison functor is an equivalence has the important consequence that
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}

admits countable colimits and is idempotent complete, see
12.3
.
We do not have a direct proof of this fact just from the construction of
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.

We start with formulating the main result of the present section.

Theorem 12.1
.

The comparison functor
h:L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is an equivalence.

As said above, we will give two proofs which should convince the reader that the assertion is true. On the other hand both involve deep facts from the classical
KK
-theory which are not easily provable on the basis of the approach taken in the present paper.

1. Proof via universal properties.

We will show that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
has the same universal property as

L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
stated in the separable version of (
11.22
). It appears as the upper horizontal equivalence in the diagram below, where
{\mathbf{D}}

is any left-exact and additive
\infty
-category:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 41.07362pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-41.07362pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{lex}}(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.94862pt\raise 4.72855pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72855pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\simeq}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.84784pt\raise-6.65819pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.68599pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.07362pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.90297pt\raise-73.81879pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.68599pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.00375pt\raise-62.43205pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{se}}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

(12.2)

where the superscript
\coprod
indicates finite coproduct preserving functors.
The lower square has been discussed in the proof of
[
BEL
, Thm.2.23]
, see
[
BEL
, (2.31)]
.
The lower horizontal equivalence reflects the fact (see
7.5
) that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is the Dwyer-Kan localization
at the set
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{se}}
of morphisms in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
inverted by
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
The crucial point is the existence of the arrow marked by
!
. To see that it exists in
[
BEL
]
we used the comparison of

\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
with the classical theory and the fact that the latter has a universal property involving the condition of split-exactness
[
BEL
, Cor. 2.4]
.
The middle
horizontal equivalence has been discussed in the proof of
[
BEL
, Thm.2.23]
. For our present purpose
we need the dashed equivalence which is obtained by an analogous argument explained in the subsequent paragraph.

All vertical arrows in the diagram above are fully faithful functors.
Since
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is homotopy invariant, stable, and Schochet- and semiexact, it is Schochet- and split-exact. Therefore the dashed arrow exists. We must show that it is essentially surjective. Thus consider a functor
F
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,splt+Sch}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})
. It gives rise to a functor
\hat{F}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})
such that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{*}\hat{F}\simeq F
.
It remains to show that
\hat{F}
is left-exact. It is clearly reduced. Every cartesian square in
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
can be represented as the image under
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
of a Schochet fibrant cartesian square, and by assumption
F
sends this square to a cartesian square in
{\mathbf{D}}
. This implies left-exactness of
\hat{F}
.
See the proof of
3.17
.
3
for an analogous argument.
∎

2.Proof based on (
11.2
).

For any two separable
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
the comparison map induces the second map in

\pi_{0}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(qA,K\otimes B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{wejogiwerfgerwfrewfwerfw}}}{{\simeq}}\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}(A,B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\to}}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(A,B)\ .

In view of (
11.2
)
it is a bijection.
Using the left-exactness of the comparison functor we can upgrade this
to obtain an isomorphism between the higher homotopy groups of mapping spaces by
inserting the suspension
S^{i}(B)
in the place of
B
for
i
in
{\rm Nature}
.
Since the comparison functor is clearly essentially surjective
it is an equivalence.
∎

Remark 12.2
.

We note that the two proofs are not independent. In order to obtain the marked arrow in (
12.2
)
we used
[
BEL
, Cor.2.4]
which is based on the universal property
of
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
as the initial functor to an additive category
which is homotopy invariant, stable and split-exact. The verification of this universal property

[
Hig87
, Thm. 4.5]

also uses the formula (
11.2
).

∎

Corollary 12.3
.

1.

The category
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
admits all countable colimits.

2.

For a countable family of separable
C^{*}
-algebras
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
we have an equivalence

\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B_{i})\simeq\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(\bigoplus_{i\in I}B_{i})\ .

3.

\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is idempotent complete and the inclusion
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}
identifies

\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
with the full subcategory of compact objects of
\mathrm{KK}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is stable by
7.2
.
3
it admits all finite colimits. For Assertion
1
it thus suffices to show that
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
admits countable coproducts. But this immediately follows from
Theorem

12.1
and
11.16
.

The same results imply also Assertion
2
.

Assertion
3
is a general fact about
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-completions of stable
\infty
-categories admitting countable colimits and thus an immediate consequence of
8.1

and Assertion
1
.
∎

By comparing the universal properties of
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}
stated in the separable version of (
11.22
) and (
7.7
) we see that

Theorem

12.1
is equivalent to the
automatic semiexactness theorem
.

Theorem 12.4
.

For every left-exact and additive
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
the canonical inclusion is an equivalence

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,se+Sch}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,splt+Sch}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})\ .

Apriori semiexactness is a much stronger condition than split-exactness.

Recall from (
5.1
) that for every exact sequence of
C^{*}
-algebras

0\to A\to B\xrightarrow{f}C\to 0

(12.3)

we have defined a map
\iota_{f}:A\to C(f)
from
A
to the mapping cone
C(f)
of
f
.
The following theorem was shown in
[
CS86
]
.

Theorem 12.5
.

For every semi-split exact sequence (
12.3
) of separable
C^{*}
-algebras the morphism

L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}(\iota_{f})
in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
is an equivalence.

In view of
5.1
the
Theorem

12.5
is equivalent to the automatic semiexactness
Theorem

12.4
and hence to

Theorem

12.1
.
Since the automatic semiexactness theorem is absolutely crucial in order to see that our construction of

\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
coincides with the classical constructions and the proof
of
Theorem

12.5
in
[
CS86
]
implicitly already uses
this comparison we must give an independent argument in order to avoid a circularity.

The remainder of the present section is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

12.5
.
We will closely follow the outline given in
the appendix of
[
CS86
]
. We reduce the argument to a single calculation
12.12
in

{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})
verifying that the composition of two explicit
candidates for the Bott element and its inverse is really the identity. We use this argument also as a chance to present a calculus
which allows to manipulate morphisms in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
by constructions with
semi-split exact sequences.

In the following discussion it is important to remember, in which categories the morphisms live. We will therefore
be more precise with the notation.
We will abbreviate
L:=L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}
. In contrast to the conventions in the rest of the text, e.g. a
C^{*}
-algebra considered as an object of
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
will be denoted by
L(A)
instead of simply by
A
.
By abusing the notation, for a morphism
f:qA\to K\otimes B
we will also use the notation
L(f)
for the induced element in

{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}(A,B)
under the map (
11.26
).

Following
[
Ska85
, Sec. 1]
and the appendix of
[
CS86
]
we start with a construction which associates to every semi-split exact sequence

{\mathcal{S}}:\qquad 0\to I\to A\xrightarrow{q}Q\to 0

(12.4)

of separable
C^{*}
-algebras a morphism

f_{{\mathcal{S}}}:L(Q)\to L(S(I))

(12.5)

in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
.
This construction is necessarily of analytic nature since it must take the existence of the cpc split and separability into account. We provide the details since we merge the approaches of
[
Ska85
, Sec. 1]
and
[
CS86
]
. In particular we want to work out in detail that the morphism
f_{{\mathcal{S}}}
is independent of the choices.

Construction 12.6
.

We fix a cpc split
s:Q\to A
. By Kasparov’s version of Stinespring’s theorem there exists a countably generated
A^{u}
-Hilbert-
C^{*}
-module
E_{0}

and a homomorphism
\phi:Q\to B(A^{u}\oplus E_{0})
such that

s(x)=P\phi(x)P
for all
x
in
Q
, where
P
in
B(A^{u}\oplus E_{0})
is the projection
onto
A^{u}
and we consider
A
as a subset of
B(A^{u}\oplus E_{0})
in the canonical way.
The point of taking
A^{u}
instead of
A
is that
P
becomes a compact operator on
A^{u}\oplus E_{0}
.

After making
E_{0}
smaller if necessary we can assume that
E_{0}
is generated as a
A^{u}
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module by the elements of the form
(1-P)\phi(x)Pa
for all
x
in
Q
and
a
in
A^{u}
. We refer to
12.7
for a sketch of a direct construction of
E_{0}
and
\phi
which explains the essence of the proof of the Stinespring theorem mentioned above.

The pair
(E_{0},\phi)
is uniquely determined up to canonical isomorphism.
Let
(E^{\prime}_{0},\phi^{\prime})
be another choice. Then we define a map

E_{0}\to E_{0}^{\prime}
sending the generator
(1-P)\phi(x)Pa
to the generator
(1-P^{\prime})\phi^{\prime}(x)P^{\prime}a
.
In order to see that this map is well-defined we note that

\displaystyle\langle\sum_{i}(1-P)\phi(x_{i})Pa_{i},\sum_{j}(1-P)\phi(x_{j})Pa_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}a_{i}^{*}P\phi(x_{i})^{*}(1-P)\phi(x_{j})Pa_{j}

(12.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}a_{i}^{*}\left(s(x_{i}^{*}x_{j})-s(x_{i})^{*}s(x_{j})\right)a_{j}

does only depend on the split
s
, but not on
E_{0}
and
\phi
.
In addition we observe that
the right-hand side takes values in the ideal
I
. Hence the
A^{u}
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module

E_{0}
becomes an
I
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module
E_{0|I}
when we restrict the right
A^{u}
-action to
I
.

Remark 12.7
.

Here is a direct construction of
E_{0}
and
\phi
starting from the datum of the split
s
.
One can consider the right
A^{u}
-module
Q\otimes A^{u}

with the
A^{u}
-valued (actually
I
-valued) scalar product

\langle x\otimes a,x^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime}\rangle:=a^{*}\left(s(x^{*}x^{\prime})-s(x)^{*}s(x^{\prime})\right)a^{\prime}\ .

Using that
s
is completely positive one checks that this is non-negative.
We then let
E_{0}
be the completion of
Q\otimes A^{u}
with respect to the induced seminorm. Note that this involves factoring out vectors with zero norm. We write suggestively
(1-P)\phi(x)Pa
for the image of
x\otimes a
in
E_{0}
.
For
y
in
Q
we then define
\phi(y)
in
B(A^{u}\oplus E_{0})
by

\phi(y)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a\\
(1-P)\phi(x)Pb\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}s(y)a+s(yx)b-s(y)s(x)b\\
(1-P)\phi(y)Pa+(1-P)\phi(yx)Pb+(1-P)\phi(y)Ps(x)b\end{array}\right)\ .

One checks that this is a
*
-homomorphism.
∎

We let
B
denote the unital subalgebra of
B(A^{u}\oplus E_{0})
generated by
\phi(Q)
and
P
. We further let
J
denote the ideal in
B
generated by
[\phi(Q),P]
.
We finally let
E_{1}
be the sub-
A^{u}
-Hilbert
C^{*}
module of
A^{u}\oplus E_{0}
generated by
J(A^{u}\oplus E_{0})
. We then have a canoncial homomorphism

B\to M(J)\to B(E_{1})
.

Note that

[\phi(x),P]=(1-P)\phi(x)P-P\phi(x)(1-P)
. Combining this formula with (
12.6
)
we see that
for
j
in
J
and all
e
and
e^{\prime}
in
A^{u}\oplus E_{0}
we have

\langle je,e^{\prime}\rangle\in I
.
Hence the scalar product of
E_{1}
takes values in
I
and
E_{1}
becomes an
I
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module
E_{1|I}
after restricting the right-module structure to
I
. Furthermore, since
J\subseteq K(A^{u}\oplus E_{0})

(because
P
was compact), we can conclude that

J\subseteq K(E_{1|I})
.
In detail, consider an element
j
in
J
. It can be approximated by finite sums

\sum_{i}\theta_{\xi_{i},\eta_{i}}
of one-dimensional operators on
A^{u}\oplus E_{0}
.
We can find members
u
and
u^{\prime}
of an approximate identity of
J
such that
uju^{\prime}
approximates
j
.
But then
j
is also approximated by the finite sums
\sum_{i}\theta_{u\xi_{i},u^{\prime,*}\eta_{i}}
of one-dimensional operators on
E_{1|I}
.

In the following we identify the suspension
S(A)
of a
C^{*}
-algebra with
C_{0}(S^{1}\setminus\{1\},A)
and
C_{b}(S^{1}\setminus\{1\},M(A))
with a subalgebra of the multiplier algebra
M(S(A))
.
We consider the family
F:S^{1}\mapsto B(E_{1|I})
given by
F(u):=P+u(1-P)
. Since
\phi
takes values in
B
we can consider
\phi
as a homomophism
Q\to B(E_{1|I})
.
We then define a homomorphism

f:qQ\to S(K(E_{1|I}))

(12.7)

whose associated homomorphism (see
11.2
) has the components

\hat{f}_{0},\hat{f}_{1}:Q\to C_{b}(S^{1}\setminus\{1\},B(E_{1|I}))\subseteq M(S(K(E_{1|I})))

given by

\hat{f}_{0}(x)(u):=\phi(x)\ ,\qquad\hat{f}_{1}(x)(u):=F(u)\phi(q)F(u)^{*}\ .

Then
\hat{f}_{0}(x)(u)-\hat{f}_{1}(x)(u)
belongs to
K(E_{1|I})
for every
u
and

\hat{f}_{0}(x)(1)-\hat{f}_{1}(x)(1)=0
. Thus
\hat{f}_{0}(x)-\hat{f}_{1}(x)
belongs to
S(K(E_{1|I}))
and
f
is well-defined. This homomorphism does not yet take values in the desired target
S(K\otimes I)
.
We will employ Kasparov’s stabilization theorem in order to produce a homomorphism

K(E_{1|I})\to K\otimes I
which is unique up to homotopy.

We let
H_{I}:=\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}I
denote the standard
I
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module.
We then have a canonical isomorphism
K(H_{I})\cong K\otimes I
.
Using that that
E_{1|I}
is countably generated (it is here where we use separability) and Kasparov’s stabilization theorem
[
Kas80
, Thm. 2]
we can choose an isomorphism
E_{1|I}\oplus H_{I}\cong H_{I}
which is unique up to homotopy since the unitary group of
B(H_{I})
is connected, even contractible.
Using this isomorphism
we get an embedding
E_{1|I}\to H_{I}
of
I
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules which is also well-defined up to homotopy. It induces a homomorphism
K(E_{1|I})\to K(H_{I})\cong K\otimes I
and hence

S(K(E_{1|I}))\to S(K\otimes I)
. Postcomposing (
12.7
) with this map
we get a map

f^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{S}}}:qQ\to S(K\otimes I)

(12.8)

which represents the desired map (
12.5
).
Up to homotopy it only depends on choice of the cpc split.
We finally see that
f_{{\mathcal{S}}}
is independent of the choice of the cpc split
since any two splits can be joined by a path.
∎

We now interpret the pre- or post-composition of
f_{{\mathcal{S}}}
with a homomorphism in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}

and its tensor product with an auxiliary
C^{*}
-algebra
in terms of operations with
semi-split exact sequences
[
Ska85
, Lem. 1.5]
.
We consider a map of semi-split exact sequences of separable
C^{*}
-algebras

\textstyle{\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{q}}
\textstyle{\tilde{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where the right square is a pull-back.

Lemma 12.8
.

We have an equivalence
f_{\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}\simeq f_{{\mathcal{S}}}\circ L(e)
.

Proof.

The split
s:Q\to A
canonically induces a split
\tilde{s}:\tilde{Q}\to\tilde{A}
.
Working with this split, by an inspection of the constructions we see that the resulting

I
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module
\tilde{E}_{1|I}
is canonically isomorphic to
E_{1|I}
.
With this identification we get an equality
f\circ q(e)=\tilde{f}:q\tilde{Q}\to S(K(E_{1|I}))
of maps in (
12.7
).
This implies that the desired equivalence.
∎

We now consider a diagram of semi-split exact sequences

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.77779pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.77779pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise Lemma 12.9
.

We have an equivalence
f_{\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}\simeq L(S(h))\circ f_{{\mathcal{S}}}
.

Proof.

The split
s:Q\to A
induces a split
\tilde{s}:=\tilde{h}\circ s:Q\to\tilde{A}
. Working with this split
we get a canonical isomorphism
\tilde{E}_{i|\tilde{I}}:=E_{i|I}\otimes_{I}\tilde{I}
.
Then the resulting map
qQ\to S(K(\tilde{E}_{1|\tilde{I}}))
in (
12.7
)
is
qQ\to S(K(E_{1|I}))\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{id}}\otimes\tilde{I}}S(K(\tilde{E}_{1|\tilde{I}}))
.
This implies the desired equivalence.
∎

In the following
\otimes
can be the minimal or the maximal tensor product.
Recall that
{\mathcal{S}}
denotes a semi-split exact sequence (
12.4
).
If
B
is any
C^{*}
-algebra, then

{\mathcal{S}}\otimes B\qquad 0\to I\otimes B\to I\otimes A\to Q\otimes B\to 0\ .

is semi-split exact again.

Lemma 12.10
.

We have an equivalence
f_{{\mathcal{S}}\otimes B}\simeq f_{{\mathcal{S}}}\otimes L(B)
.

Proof.

The split
s
induces a split
s\otimes B
. With this choice
the composition

qQ\otimes B\xrightarrow{\mathrm{can}}q(Q\otimes B)\xrightarrow{!}S(K\otimes I\otimes B)\cong S(K\otimes I)\otimes B

with the marked map constructed from
{\mathcal{S}}\otimes B

is equal to the map
f\otimes B
constructed from
s
, and the morphism
\mathrm{can}
induces an equivalence in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
since
L
is symmetric monoidal.
This implies the assertion.
∎

The semi-split exact exact sequence

\mathcal{R}:\qquad 0\to S({\mathbb{C}})\to C({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}\to 0\ .

(12.9)

gives rise to a map
f_{\mathcal{R}}:L({\mathbb{C}})\to L(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))
in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
.
The crucial fact is that it admits
a left inverse, a posteriori even an inverse.
This left inverse
L(\beta):L(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))\to L({\mathbb{C}})
, called the
Bott element
,
will be given by an explicit homomorphism
\beta:qS^{2}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(K)
in
12.17
.

Remark 12.11
.

In the reference
[
CS86
]
the map
f_{\mathcal{R}}
is called the Bott element. We prefer to call
\beta
the Bott element because of its role in
7.2
.
It is actually the crucial point that the Bott element
\beta
constructed in
6.9

in the semiexact situation has a lift to the split-exact world considered in the present section.
This fact is the heart of the automatic semiexactness.
∎

Proposition 12.12
.

We have
L(\beta)\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\simeq\pm{\mathtt{id}}_{L({\mathbb{C}})}
.

Remark 12.13
.

12.12
is shown in
[
CS86
]
by calculating a Kasparov product.
This argument is therefore not part of the theory developed in the present note.

Of course, the obvious approach would be to calculate the composition of the two representatives explicitly.
This would result in a map
q^{2}{\mathbb{C}}\to K\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
which would have to be compared with the composition of a left upper corner inclusion
{\mathbb{C}}\to K
with
\iota_{{\mathbb{C}}}\circ\iota_{q{\mathbb{C}}}:q^{2}{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. To go this path seems to be quite tricky.

Further below we will therefore provide argument for
12.12
which avoids to go through Kasparov products or making the homomorphism
\phi
in
Theorem

11.13
explicit.

∎

For the moment we assume
12.12
. For simplicity we will adjust the sign of
\beta
such that

L(\beta)\circ f_{\mathcal{R}}\simeq{\mathtt{id}}_{L({\mathbb{C}})}
.

Proof of
Theorem

12.5
.

We reproduce the argument from the appendix of
[
CS86
]
.
We consider a semi-split exact sequence

{\mathcal{S}}:\qquad 0\to I\to A\xrightarrow{q}Q\to 0

and the map
\iota_{q}:I\to C(q)
as in (
5.1
).
We want to show that
L(\iota_{q})
is an equivalence.

We have a semi-split exact sequence

{\mathcal{T}}:\qquad 0\to S(I)\to C(A)\to C(q)\to 0\ ,

where the second map sends an element of
C(A)
given by a path
\sigma
in
A
with
\sigma(1)=0
to
the pair
(\sigma(0),q\circ\sigma)
in
C(q)
, see
3.16
. The kernel of the map consists of paths
\sigma
in
I
with
\sigma(0)=0=\sigma(1)
and is hence isomorphic to
S(I)
.
We let
f_{{\mathcal{T}}}:L(C(q))\to L(S^{2}(I))
be the associated morphism.
Then we define

u:=(L(\beta)\otimes L(I))\circ f_{{\mathcal{T}}}:L(C(q))\to L(I)\ .

(12.10)

We now calculate the composition

u\circ L(\iota_{q}):L(I)\to L(I)
.
Using (
12.9
)
we have a map of exact sequences

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes I:}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S(I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{q}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S(I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where the right square is a pull-back.
Then
by
12.8
and
12.10
we have

f_{{\mathcal{T}}}\circ L(\iota_{q})\simeq f_{\mathcal{R}\otimes I}\simeq f_{\mathcal{R}}\otimes L(I):L(I)\to L(S^{2}(I))\ .

(12.11)

Using
12.12
we conclude that

\displaystyle u\circ L(\iota_{q})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wergerwffrfrffwe}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle(L(\beta)\otimes L(I))\circ f_{{\mathcal{T}}}\circ L(\iota_{q})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wergpokpregwfewff}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle(L(\beta)\otimes L(I))\circ(f_{\mathcal{R}}\otimes L(I))

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle(L(\beta)\circ f_{\mathcal{R}})\otimes L(I)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fkjgogerfwreferfw}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle{\mathtt{id}}_{L(I)}\ .

Hence
\iota_{q}
is a split monomorphism with left-inverse
u
.

We have the semi-split exact exact sequence

{\mathcal{U}}:\qquad 0\to C(\iota_{q})\to C(C(q))\xrightarrow{\phi}C(Q)\to 0\ .

Using
3.16

recall that
C(C(q))
consists of pairs
(\sigma,\gamma)
with
\sigma
a path in
A
,
\gamma=(\gamma(-,t))_{t}
a path of paths in
Q

such that
\gamma(s,1)=0
for all
s
,
\gamma(1,t)=0
for all
t
,
q(\sigma(s))=\gamma(s,0)
for all
s
and
\sigma(1)=0
.
The map
\phi
sends
(\sigma,\gamma)
to
\gamma(0,-)
. Its kernel consists
pairs
(\sigma,\gamma)
where
\sigma
is a path in
A
with
\sigma(0)\in I
and
\sigma(1)=0
, and
\gamma
is path of paths in
Q
with
\gamma(0,t)=0
,
\gamma(1,t)=0
,
q(\sigma(s))=\gamma(s,0)
, and
\gamma(s,1)=0
for all
s,t
. This is precisely the description of a point in
C(\iota_{q})
.

Applying the above to the semi-split exact sequence
{\mathcal{U}}

we conclude that
L(\iota_{\phi}):L(C(\iota_{q}))\to L(C(\phi))
is a split monomorphism.
Since
L
is Schochet exact we see that
L(C(\phi))\simeq 0
since it is the fibre of a map between objects which are equivalent to zero (cones are contractible).
This implies that
L(C(\iota_{q}))\simeq 0
.

Again using that
L
is Schochet exact we can conclude that
L(S(\iota_{q})):L(S(I))\to L(S(C(q)))
is an equivalence.
Then also
L(S^{2}(\iota_{q}))
is an equivalence. But
L(\iota_{q})
is a retract of
L(S^{2}(\iota_{q}))
by

\textstyle{L(I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{id}}_{L(I)}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathcal{R}}\otimes L(I)}
\scriptstyle{L(\iota_{q})}
\textstyle{L(S^{2}(I))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta\otimes L(I)}
\scriptstyle{L(S^{2}(\iota_{q}))}
\textstyle{L(I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L(\iota_{q})}
\textstyle{C(q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{id}}_{L(C(q))}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathcal{R}}\otimes L(C(q))}
\textstyle{L(S^{2}(C(q)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta\otimes L(C(q))}
\textstyle{L(C(q))}

and hence an equivalence, too.
∎

Assume that

{\mathcal{S}}:\qquad 0\to I\to A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\to}}Q\to 0

is semi-split exact. By semiexactness of
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}

we get a boundary map
\partial_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\mathrm{kk}}:\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(S(Q))\to\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}(I)
. By the automatic
semiexactness theorem we know that there is a boundary map
\partial_{{\mathcal{S}}}:L(S(Q))\to L(I)
such that
h(\partial_{{\mathcal{S}}})\simeq\partial_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\mathrm{kk}}
, where
h
is the left-exact comparison functor in (
12.1
).
The following proposition clarifies its relation with
f_{{\mathcal{S}}}:L(Q)\to L(S(I))
stated in
the last sentence of
[
CS86
]
.

Recall the exact sequence (
12.9
).

.

We have an equivalence

It implies by
12.9
that

L(S(\iota_{q}))\circ f_{{\mathcal{S}}}\simeq f_{{\mathcal{U}}}\simeq L(S(\partial_{q}))\circ(f_{\mathcal{R}}\otimes L(Q))\ .

We now use that
L(\iota_{q})
is an equivalence and that

L(\iota_{q})\circ\partial_{{\mathcal{S}}}\simeq L(\partial_{q})
in order to deduce the
desired equivalence.
∎

We now prepare the proof of
12.12
.
We will freely
use results from
Section

9
and
Section

10
.
We start with showing a partial case of
Theorem

12.1
.

Lemma 12.15
.

For any separable
C^{*}
-algebra
B
the comparison functor
h
in (
12.1
) induces an equivalence

{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},B)\xrightarrow{h}\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathbb{C}},B)\ .

Proof.

Replacing
\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to E
by
L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}
and correspondingly

\Omega^{\infty}K(-)
by
{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},-)
we can construct
a natural transformation

\tilde{q}_{\mathrm{sep},h}:\mathcal{P}\tilde{\mathrm{roj}}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{s}(-)\to{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},-)

in complete analogy to the construction of
\tilde{\mathrm{e}}_{h}
in (
10.6
).
We start from

q_{h}:=L_{\mathrm{sep},q}:\mathcal{P}\mathrm{roj}^{s}(-)\to{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},-)

in place of
e_{h}
in (
10.5
), use that
L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}
is split-exact and that the unitalization sequence (
10.3
) is split-exact, and that
{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},-)
takes values in groups.
The proof of
10.9
goes through word by word and shows that

\tilde{q}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
is an equivalence. Here instead of the (
7.9
) we use of course the universal property of
L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}
given by the separable version of (
11.22
)
in order to construct the factorization
\mathcal{P}\bar{\mathrm{roj}}:L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}\to\mathbf{CGroups}(\mathbf{Spc})
in the analogue of (
10.14
). The commutativity of

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\tilde{\mathrm{roj}}^{s}_{\mathrm{sep}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\mathrm{e}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{q}_{\mathrm{sep},h}}
\textstyle{{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathbb{C}},B)}

implies that
h
is also an equivalence.
∎

Example 12.16
.

By the calculation of the spectrum
KU\simeq\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})
in
9.19
(and implicitly using
9.16
.
2
in order to go from
E
- to
KK
-theory)
we know that for any
i
in
{\rm Nature}

\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},S^{i}({\mathbb{C}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{qerijgoqrfqdfewfqef}}}{{\cong}}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}({\mathbb{C}},S^{i}({\mathbb{C}}))\cong\pi_{i}\mathrm{KU}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qefoihwefioewdqewdqwedwqedwqedwdwed}}}{{\cong}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathbb{Z}}&i\in 2{\rm Nature}\\
0&else\end{array}\right.\ .

The proof of
12.15
together with the fact that
\pi_{0}
sends the homotopy theoretic group completion to the algebraic group completion implies that

\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})\to\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})

is the algebraic group completion. This will allow us to detect elements in the group
\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})

represented by maps
{\mathbb{C}}\to K
or
q{\mathbb{C}}\to K
in a simple manner.

We identify maps
p:{\mathbb{C}}\to K
with the projections
p
in
K
given by the image of
1
.
A map
p:{\mathbb{C}}\to K
is determined up to homotopy by the dimension
\dim(p)
of the range of
p
. We therefore have an isomorphism of monoids

\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})\to{\rm Nature}\ ,\quad p\mapsto\dim(p)\ .

We now consider a map
(p_{0},p_{1}):q{\mathbb{C}}\to K
given in terms of an associated map with components
p_{i}:{\mathbb{C}}\to M(K)=B
for
i=0,1

such that
p_{0}-p_{1}\in K
. Then
p_{1}p_{0}:{\mathtt{im}}(p_{0})\to{\mathtt{im}}(p_{1})
is a Fredholm operator
and we can define the relative index

I(p_{0},p_{1}):={\mathtt{index}}(p_{1}p_{0}:{\mathtt{im}}(p_{0})\to{\mathtt{im}}(p_{1}))\ .

If
p_{i}
is compact for
i=0,1
, then
I(p_{0},p_{1})=\dim(p_{0})-\dim(p_{1})
.

We now show that the relative index is homotopy invariant for homotopies of pairs
(p_{0,t},p_{1,t})
such that
p_{0,t}-p_{1,t}
is norm continuous. In particular there is no continuity condition on the familes
p_{i,t}
separately.
We follow
[
ASS94
]
and define the norm continuous families of selfadjoint operators
A_{t}:=p_{0,t}-p_{1,t}
and
B_{t}:=1-A_{t}
. Then
A^{2}_{t}+B^{2}_{t}=0
and
A_{t}B_{t}+B_{t}A_{t}=0
(see
[
ASS94
, Thm. 2.1]
). Since
A_{t}
is selfadjoint and compact the spectrum of
A_{t}
away from
0
is discrete and consists of eigenvalues of finite multiplicity. The relations above imply that
for
\lambda\not=1
the operator
B_{t}
induces an isomorphism between
{\mathtt{ker}}(A_{t}-\lambda)
and
{\mathtt{ker}}(A_{t}+\lambda)
. By
[
ASS94
, Prop. 3.1]
we have the first equality in

\displaystyle{\mathtt{index}}(p_{0,t},p_{1,t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim({\mathtt{ker}}(A_{t}-1))-\dim({\mathtt{ker}}(A_{t}+1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim(E_{A_{t}}((1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)))-\dim(E_{A_{t}}((-1-\epsilon,-1+\epsilon)))

for any
\epsilon
in
(0,1)
, where
E_{A_{t}}
is the family of spectral projections for
A_{t}
. In order to see the second equality note that the contributions of the eigenspaces to the eigenvalues different from
\pm 1
cancel out by the consideration above.
The right-hand side is continuous in
t
and hence constant. To this end, we consider a point
t_{0}
in
[0,1]
. Then we choose
\epsilon
such that

1\pm\epsilon
do not belong to the spectrum of
A_{t_{0}}
.
By the norm continuity of
t\mapsto A_{t}
there exists
\delta
in
(0,\infty)

for all
t
in

[t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta]\cap[0,1]
the points
1\pm\epsilon
do not belong to the spectrum
of
A_{t}
. But then the right-hand side is constant on
[t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta]\cap[0,1]
.

Using the homotopy invariance and the additivity of the relative index for block sums we can conclude that
the map
(p_{0},p_{1})\mapsto I(p_{0},p_{1})

induces a group homomorphism
\tilde{\dim}:\pi_{0}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(q{\mathbb{C}},K)\to{\mathbb{Z}}
such that the bold part of

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\pi_{0}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}({\mathbb{C}},K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}L}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\dim}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm Nature}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(q{\mathbb{C}},K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\dim}}
\scriptstyle{!}
(
11.26
)
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

commutes.
The dashed arrow is obtained from the universal property of the arrow denoted by
\pi_{0}L
as a group completion, since
the right-down map
i\circ\dim
is a homomorphism to a group.
It remains to show that the lower triangle commutes.

We claim that the arrow marked by
!
is an isomorphism.
Assuming the claim we know that
\iota^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
also represents a group completion.
We can then argue
that the two ways to go from
\pi_{0}\underline{{\mathtt{Hom}}}(q{\mathbb{C}},K)
to
{\mathbb{Z}}
must agree since
group completions are initial in homomorphisms to groups.

In order to see the claim we can appeal to
11.14
.
But as this implicitly uses
Theorem

11.13
one could alternatively
show directly that
\tilde{\dim}
is an isomorphism and then conclude that
!
is an isomorphism.

∎

Construction 12.17
.

In this construction we describe the Bott element
L(\beta)
in the mapping space
{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathbb{C}})
. Instead of reproducing the construction from
[
CS86
]
we describe a version which is more amenable to explicit calculations.

We consider the closed smooth manifold
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cong S^{2}
. It will be equipped
with
a constant scalar curvature Riemannian metric and the orientation determined by the complex structure. We consider
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
as a
Riemannian spin manifold and let
\not{D}
be the spin Dirac operator.
It acts as a first order elliptic differential operator on the sections of the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded spinor bundle
S\cong S^{+}\oplus S^{-}
which is odd with respect to the grading and therefore represented by a matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\not{D}^{-}\\
\not{D}^{+}&0\end{array}\right)\ .

(12.12)

The Schrödinger-Lichnerowicz formula states that
\not{D}^{2}=\Delta+\frac{s}{4}
, where
\Delta
is the canonical Laplacian on the spinor bundle associated to the connection and
s
is the scalar curvature. Since the Laplacian is non-negative and
s
is positive we see that
\not{D}^{2}
is positive and hence invertible as an unbounded operator on
H:=L^{2}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1},S)
with domain
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1},S)
. Using function calculus we obtain the odd and zero order pseudodifferential unitary operator

U:=\not{D}|\not{D}|^{-1}

in
B(H)
.
The principal symbol of
U
is the unitary part of the polar decomposition of the principal symbol of
\not{D}
.
If
f
in
C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
acts as multiplication operator on
H
, then
[f,U]
is compact.
Indeed, let
f
be smooth for the moment and consider it as a zero order pseudodifferential operator. Then the principal symbols of
f
and
U
commute and the commutator is a pseudodifferential
operator of order
-1
and hence compact. Since
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
is dense in
C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
in the norm and the compact operators are closed in norm we see that
[f,U]
is compact for all
f
in
C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
.

The grading of
S
gives a decomposition
H=H^{+}\oplus H^{-}
and we represent
U
as a matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&U^{-}\\
U^{+}&0\end{array}\right)\ .

We have two homomorphisms
\hat{\phi}_{\pm}:C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\to B(H^{\pm})
such that for
f
in
C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
the operator
\hat{\phi}_{\pm}(f)
is the multiplication operator by
f
on
H^{\pm}
.

We define two homomorphisms
\hat{\phi}_{i}:C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\to B(H^{+})
for
i=0,1
by

\hat{\phi}_{0}:=\hat{\phi}_{+}\ ,\quad\hat{\phi}_{1}:=U^{-}\hat{\phi}_{-}U^{+}\ .

(12.13)

Then we have
\hat{\phi}_{0}(f)-\hat{\phi}_{1}(f)\in K(H^{+})

for all
f
in
C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
. The homomorphisms
\hat{\phi}_{i}
for
i=0,1
are therefore the components of the associated homomorphism of a homomorphism

\hat{\beta}:qC({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\to K(H^{+})\ .

It represents a point
L(\hat{\beta})
in
{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}(C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}),{\mathbb{C}})
.

We fix a base point
*
in
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Using an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\cong{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\setminus\{*\}
we identify
S^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
with the subalgebra
C_{0}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\setminus\{*\})

of
C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
of functions vanishing at
*
. We let
\iota:S^{2}({\mathbb{C}})\to C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
denote the inclusion.
We define the Bott element as the composition

\beta:qS^{2}({\mathbb{C}})\xrightarrow{q(\iota)}qC({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\xrightarrow{\hat{\beta}}K(H^{+})\ .

Then
L(\beta)\simeq L(\hat{\beta})\circ L(\iota)
is a point in
\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathbb{C}})
which is our candidate for the Bott element.

∎

Proof of
12.12
.

We have the following commutative diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 127.01846pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-127.01846pt\raise
