133.96985pt\raise 4.62848pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.01846pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 151.01846pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 203.62492pt\raise-16.32pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72855pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\simeq}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 203.62492pt\raise-24.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-96.80708pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathbb{C}})\times\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathbb{C}},S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.69478pt\raise-28.01154pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.6797pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 166.6797pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ ,

(12.14)

where the vertical morphisms are induced by
h
and the horizontal morphisms are given by composition.
The right vertical morphism is an equivalence by
12.15
.

We have

\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathbb{C}}))\cong\pi_{-2}\mathrm{KU}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
. We furthermore know that under the identification

\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(S^{i}({\mathbb{C}}),S^{j}({\mathbb{C}}))\cong\pi_{j-i}\mathrm{KU}
the composition

\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(S^{j}({\mathbb{C}}),S^{k}({\mathbb{C}}))\times\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(S^{i}({\mathbb{C}}),S^{j}({\mathbb{C}}))\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(S^{i}({\mathbb{C}}),S^{k}({\mathbb{C}}))

is identified with the
multiplication in the ring
\pi_{*}\mathrm{KU}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[b,b^{-1}]
.

So in order to show that
L(\beta)
is a left-inverse inverse of
f_{\mathcal{R}}
up to sign it suffices to show
that the images
h(L(\beta))
and
h(f_{\mathcal{R}})
of these elements in
\pi_{-2}\mathrm{KU}
and
\pi_{2}\mathrm{KU}
are generators.
This is the content of
12.18
and
12.19
.

Lemma 12.18
.

The class
h(L(\beta))
in
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathbb{C}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
is a generator.

Proof.

It suffices to provide
an element

L(p)
in
{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))
such that
L(\beta)\circ L(p)
represents a generator
of
\pi_{0}{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We keep the conventions from
12.17
.
We have a tautological line bundle
L\to{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
which is naturally a subbundle of the trivial bundle
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. We let
L^{\perp}
be the orthogonal complement so that

L\oplus L^{\perp}\cong{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. We consider the projection
P

in
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}))
such that the value
P_{x}
is the orthogonal projection onto the fibre of
L^{\perp}_{x}
of
L^{\perp}
for all
x
in
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. We interpret
P
as a homomorphism
P:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}))
such that
1\mapsto P
.
We get
L(P)
in
{\mathtt{Map}}_{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}}({\mathbb{C}},C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}))
.
We can now calculate the composition

L(\hat{\beta})\circ L(P)
which is represented by

q{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{qP}q{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}))\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(\hat{\beta})}{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(K(H^{+}))\ ,

(12.15)

where
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(\hat{\beta})
is the map whose associated homomorphism has the components
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(\hat{\phi}_{i})
with
\hat{\phi}_{i}
as in (
12.13
).
We set
\hat{H}:=H^{+}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
and identify
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(K(H^{+}))\cong K(\hat{H})
.
The components of the map (
12.15
) are then given by the projections

Q_{i}:={\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(\hat{\phi}_{i})(P)
in
B(\hat{H})
.
Note that the difference
Q_{0}-Q_{1}
is compact. By
12.16
the class of the composition in (
12.15
) is detected by the relative index

I(Q_{0},Q_{1})
on
{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e., the index of the Fredholm operator
Q_{1}Q_{0}:Q_{0}\hat{H}\to Q_{1}\hat{H}
.
We note that
Q_{0}=P_{+}
and
Q_{1}={\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(U^{-})P_{-}{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(U^{+})
, where we consider
P_{\pm}
as a multiplication operator
P
on
H^{\pm}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\cong L^{2}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1},S^{\pm}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
.
Multiplying with the unitary
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(U^{-})
from the left we can thus identify the Fredholm operator
Q_{1}Q_{0}
with

P_{-}{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(U^{+})P_{+}:L^{2}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1},S^{+}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1},S^{-}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\ .

This is a zero order pseudodifferential operator whose symbol is the
symbol of the twisted Dirac operator

\not{D}^{+}_{L}
(see (
12.12
)) made unitary. In particular
we have
I(Q_{0},Q_{1})={\mathtt{index}}(\not{D}^{+}_{L})
.
By the Atiyah-Singer index theorem we have

{\mathtt{index}}(\not{D}^{+}_{L})=\int_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\hat{A}(S^{2}){\mathbf{ch}}(L^{\perp})=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}c_{1}(L^{\perp})=1\ .

The base point of
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
gives a decomposition
L_{*}\oplus L_{*}^{\perp}\cong{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
.
We let
P_{*}
in
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}))
be the corresponding constant projection onto
L_{*}^{\perp}
.
The same calculations as above show that the composition
L(\hat{\beta})\circ L(P_{*})
represented by

q{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{qP_{*}}q{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C(S^{2}))\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(\hat{\beta})}{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(K(H^{+}))

represents the zero element.

The projections
P
and
P_{*}
in
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C(S^{2}))\cong M({\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}})))
can be considered
as components of the associated homomorphism of a homomorphism

p:q{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))
since
P-P_{*}\in{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))
.
The composition

q{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{p}{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(\iota)}{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C(S^{2}))

represents the difference, i.e.,

L(\iota)\circ L(p)\simeq L(P)-L(P_{*})
.
This implies that

L(\beta)\circ L(p)\simeq L(\hat{\beta})\circ L(\iota)\circ L(p)\simeq L(\hat{\beta})\circ(L(P)-L(P_{*}))\simeq L(\hat{\beta})\circ L(P)\simeq L({\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbb{C}}})

is a generator.
∎

Lemma 12.19
.

The class
h(f_{\mathcal{R}})
in
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}({\mathbb{C}},S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
is a generator.

Proof.

We first
make
12.6
explicit in order to
describe an explicit representative of the map
f_{\mathcal{R}}
.
Let
t
be the coordinate on
[0,1]
. We identify the cone over
{\mathbb{C}}
as
C({\mathbb{C}})\cong C_{0}((0,1])
. We define the cpc map

s:{\mathbb{C}}\to C({\mathbb{C}})
such that
s(1)=t
, where
t
is the coordinate function of the interval acting by multiplication on
C_{0}((0,1])
.
We consider
E_{0}:=S({\mathbb{C}})\cong C_{0}((0,1))
as a
C({\mathbb{C}})^{u}
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module.
We use the identifcation
C({\mathbb{C}})^{u}\cong C([0,1])
and
define

\phi:{\mathbb{C}}\to B(C([0,1])\oplus E_{0})
such that

\phi(1)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}t&\sqrt{t(1-t)}\\
\sqrt{t(1-t)}&(1-t)\end{array}\right)\ .

Note that the right-hand side is a projection.
We set

P:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right)\ .

Then
(1-P)\phi(1)Pa=a\sqrt{t(1-t)}
for
a
in
C([0,1])
.
These elements span a dense subset of
E_{0}
.
The unital algebra
B
spanned by
P
and
\phi(1)
is given by

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}C([0,1])&S({\mathbb{C}})\\
S({\mathbb{C}})&C(S^{1})\end{array}\right)\subseteq B(C([0,1])\oplus S({\mathbb{C}}))\ .

The ideal
J
generated by
[P,\phi(1)]
is
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(S({\mathbb{C}}))
, and
E_{1}=S({\mathbb{C}})\oplus S({\mathbb{C}})
.
We get a map
q{\mathbb{C}}\to S({\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(S({\mathbb{C}})))
whose associated map has the components

\hat{f}_{0}(1)(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}t&\sqrt{t(1-t)}\\
\sqrt{t(1-t)}&(1-t)\end{array}\right)\ ,\quad\hat{f}_{0}(1)(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}t&u^{-1}\sqrt{t(1-t)}\\
u\sqrt{t(1-t)}&(1-t)\end{array}\right)\ .

This map represents
f_{\mathcal{R}}
.

In order to check that
h(f_{\mathcal{R}})
is a generator, using Swan’s theorem we will translate the problem into a calculation in usual topological
K
-theory
of compact spaces defined in terms of vector bundles.
We interpret
t
and
u
as longitude and latitude coordinates on
S^{2}
such that
t=0
is the south pole and
t=1
is the north pole. By Swan’s theorem we have an isomorphism between

\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}({\mathbb{C}},C(S^{2}))\cong K_{0}(C(S^{2}))
and the
K
-theory
K^{0}(S^{2})
of the sphere defined in terms of vector bundles as usual.
Under this isomorphism and with
S^{2}({\mathbb{C}})\cong C_{0}(S^{2}\setminus\{n\})
we identify
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},0}({\mathbb{C}},S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))
with the reduced
K
-theory
\tilde{K}^{0}(S^{2})
relative to the north pole
\{n\}
.

Since
\hat{f}_{0}(1)
does not depend on
u
it is obviously a projection
P_{0}
in
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C(S^{2}))
. We now observe that
\hat{f}_{1}(1)
does not depend on
u
if
t=0
or
t=1
. We can therefore also interpret
\hat{f}_{1}(1)
as a projection
P_{1}
in
{\mathtt{Mat}}_{2}(C(S^{2}))
. Identifying projections with vector bundles (actually subbundles of the trivial bundle
S^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
) we get a class
[P_{0}]-[P_{1}]
in
K^{0}(S^{2})
.
Since
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
coincide at the north pole this difference is actually a reduced class
in
\tilde{K}^{0}(S^{2})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
. Our task is to show that it is a generator.

Since
P_{0}
does not depend on the
u
-coordinate
it comes from a projection in
C([0,1])
. Since
[0,1]
is contractible
we can conclude that
P_{0}
is homotopic to a constant projection and the corresponding vector bundle can be trivialized. The matrix function

(t,u)\mapsto U(t,u):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&u\end{array}\right)

defines an isomorphism of vector bundles
P_{0}\to P_{1}
(considered as subbundles of the two-dimensional trivial bundle)
on the subspace
\{t\not=0\}
. Since

P_{0,n}={\mathbb{C}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\right)

this isomorphism extends across the north pole. Away from the north pole this isomorphism sends the section

(u,t)\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{t(1-t)}\\
(1-t)\end{array}\right)

of
P_{0}

to the section

(u,t)\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{t(1-t)}\\
u(1-t)\end{array}\right)\ .

Note that if
t
becomes small this is essentially multiplication by
u
.
So
P_{1}
is obtained from the trivial bundle
by cutting at the equator
S^{1}\subseteq S^{2}
and regluing with a map
S^{1}\to U(1)
of degree one.
This implies that
[P_{0}]-[P_{1}]
generates
\tilde{K}^{0}(S^{2})
.
∎

This finishes the proof of
12.12
.
∎

13
Half-exact functors

In classical
KK
- and
E
-theory universal properties are formulated in terms of half-exact functors to ordinary additive categories. In the present section we will recall this language and state the universal properties of the homotopy category versions of the functors constructed in the previous sections in these terms. This will be used to show that they are equivalent to the classical
KK
- and
E
-theory functors. This comparison is the main objective of this section.
For simplicity we will restrict to separable algebras.

Recall that an additive
1
-category is an ordinary category which is pointed, admits finite coproducts and products such for any two objects
D,D^{\prime}
the canonical morphism
D\sqcup D^{\prime}\to D\times D^{\prime}
is an isomorphism, and which has the property that the commutative monoids
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(D,D^{\prime})
are abelian groups for all objects
D,D^{\prime}
. An additive category is automatically enriched in abelian groups.
A functor
{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
between additive categories is additive if it preserves coproducts and products.
It is then compatible with the enrichments in abelian groups. We let
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{add}}({\mathbf{D}},{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})
be the category of additive functors.

Example 13.1
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is an additive
\infty
-category, then its homotopy category
\mathrm{ho}{\mathbf{C}}
is an additive
1
-category.

∎

We next introduce the notion of a half-exact additive category. A half-exact structure looks like a
glimpse of a triangulated structure. We will use this notion mainly in order to
match the formulation of the universal properties of
KK
- and
E
-theory in the classical literature, in particular
in
[
Hig90a
]
.

Let
A\to B\to C
be a sequence of maps in an additive
1
-category
{\mathbf{D}}

and
{\mathcal{S}}
be a set of objects of
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Definition 13.2
.

We say that this sequence is half-exact with respect to
{\mathcal{S}}
if the induced sequences

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(D,A)\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(D,B)\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(D,C)

and

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(C,D)\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(B,D)\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(A,D)

of abelian groups are exact for every
D
in
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Definition 13.3
.

A marking on an additive
1
-category is a subset of objects
{\mathcal{S}}
which is closed under isomorphisms.
A half-exact additive category is an additive
1
-category with a marking and a collection of distinguished sequences
which are required to be half-exact with respect to the given marking .

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
be half-exact additive categories with markings
{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
, respectively.

Definition 13.4
.

A half-exact functor
\phi:{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
is an additive functor such that
\phi({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
and
\phi
sends the distinguished half-exact sequences in
{\mathbf{D}}
to distinguished half-exact sequences in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
.

We let
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{add},\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{ex}}({\mathbf{D}},{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})
be the category of half-exact additive functors between half-exact additive categories.

Example 13.5
.

Every additive category has the
canonical half-exact structure
with
{\mathcal{S}}=\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{D}})
and
the collection of all sequences which are half-exact with respect to this marking.
In this case we denote the half-exact additive category by
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{can}}
.
∎

Example 13.6
.

Recall that a sequence
A\xrightarrow{i}B\xrightarrow{p}C
in an additive
1
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
is
split-exact
if
p
admits a left inverse
s:C\to B
such that
(i,s):A\oplus C\to B
is an isomorphism. The split-exact sequences are half-exact for the maximal marking.
We write
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{splt}}
for
{\mathbf{D}}
equipped with the maximal marking and the collection of
split-exact sequences.
An additive functor
{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
automatically preserves split-exact sequences.
It therefore belongs to

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{add},\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{ex}}({\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{splt}},{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{splt}})
.
∎

Remark 13.7
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
be a half-exact additive category with marking
{\mathcal{S}}
. Then a sequence

D_{0}\to D_{1}\to D_{2}\to D_{3}\to D_{4}

in
{\mathbf{D}}
is called half-exact exact if each segment
D_{i-1}\to D_{i}\to D_{i+1}
is half-exact. If
D_{0}\to D_{1}
and
D_{3}\to D_{4}
are isomorphisms and
D_{2}
belongs to
{\mathcal{S}}
, then we can conclude that
D_{2}\cong 0
by showing that
{\mathtt{id}}_{D_{2}}=0
.

If
D_{1}\cong 0
and
D_{4}\cong 0
and
D_{2}
and
D_{3}
belong to
{\mathcal{S}}
, then we can conclude that

D_{2}\to D_{3}
is an isomorphism by constructing left and right inverses.
∎

Example 13.8
.

In order to have a non-trivial half-exact structure at hand consider the category of abelian groups and let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the set of uniquely divisible abelian groups. We distinguish all sequences which are half-exact with respect to
{\mathcal{S}}
.
Then e.g. the sequence
0\to{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{5}{\mathbb{Z}}\to 0
is half-exact.
Note that in this case we can not conclude that
5
is an isomorphism since
{\mathbb{Z}}
does not belong to the marking.

∎

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
be a half-exact additive category.
A functor
F:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
from a left-exact
\infty
-category will be called
half-exact
if the marking of
{\mathbf{D}}
contains
F(\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{C}}))

and
F

sends
fibre sequences to distinguished half-exact sequences.

Definition 13.9
.

A functor

F:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is called half-exact (half-semiexact) if the marking of
{\mathbf{D}}
contains
F(\mathrm{Ob}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}))
, and if
F
sends
exact (semi-split exact) sequences to distinguished half-exact sequences. A functor
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is split-exact if it sends split-exact sequences of
C^{*}
-algebras to split-exact sequences.

We indicate such functors by superscripts
\frac{1}{2}ex
,
\frac{1}{2}se
, or
splt
.

Example 13.10
.

If

{\mathbf{C}}
is a stable
\infty
-category, then
\mathrm{ho}{\mathbf{C}}
is a triangulated
1
-category.
The sequences
A\to B\to C
for any distinguished triangle
A\to B\to C\to\Sigma A
are
half-exact with respect to the maximal marking.
We call the half-exact structure consisting of the maximal marking and these sequences the
triangulated half-exact structure
.
The corresponding half-exact additive category will be denoted by

\mathrm{ho}{\mathbf{C}}_{\Delta}
.

If we consider
{\mathbf{C}}
as a left-exact
\infty
-category, then according to our conventions the functor
\mathrm{ho}:{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathrm{ho}{\mathbf{C}}_{\Delta}
is half-exact.
∎

We consider
!
in
\{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{se},q\}
. For
!\in\{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{se}\}
the functor

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
is as in (
8.4
). For
!=q

we set

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}:=L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,q}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}:=L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}

using the separable versions of (
11.11
).
We note that the targets of these functors are a left-exact additive
\infty
-category for
!=q
(separable version of
11.8
.
3
) or even stable
\infty
-categories (
7.3
.
3
) for
!\in\{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{se}\}
. In particular, their homotopy categories are additive
1
-categories.
We consider the functor

\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}:=\mathrm{ho}\circ\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}\ .

We will equip the additive
1
-category
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
with the triangulated half-exact structure in case
!\in\{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{se}\}
, and the split
half-exact structure if
!=q
. In all cases the marking is the maximal one.

Corollary 13.11
.

The functor
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
is homotopy invariant and stable. Moreover, it is split-exact in case
!=q
, half-semiexact in case
!=\mathrm{se}
, and half-exact in case
!=\mathrm{ex}
.

Proof.

Homotopy invariance and stability are clear by construction.

In case
!=\mathrm{ex}
(
!=\mathrm{se}
) we use that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
sends
exact (semi-split exact) sequences of
C^{*}
-algebras to fibre sequences, and the half-exactness of
\mathrm{ho}
.

In case
!=q
we use that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
and
\mathrm{ho}
preserve split-exact sequences.
∎

In order to make uniform statements in all three cases we will call a functor
F:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
to a half-exact additive category
!
-exact if it is split-exact in case
!=q
, half-semiexact in case
!=\mathrm{se}
, or exact in case
!=\mathrm{ex}
. We furthermore call the functor
suspension stable
if for every morphism
f:A\to B
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
the fact that
F(f)
is an isomorphism is equivalent to the fact that
F(S(f))
is an isomorphism.

Example 13.12
.

For
!
in
\{\mathrm{se},\mathrm{ex}\}

the functors
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
are suspension stable.
This fact is due to the triangulated structure on
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
which is a consequence if the stability of
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
. We further use
3.19
in order to identify looping in
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
with suspension on the level of algebras.
∎

We let
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
denote the set of morphisms in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
which are sent to equivalences by
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
.

We consider a half-exact additive category
{\mathbf{D}}
and a functor
F:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
.

Proposition 13.13
.

If
F
is
homotopy invariant, stable, (suspension stable in the cases
!\in\{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{se})\}
), and
!
-exact, then
F
sends
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
to isomorphisms

Proof.

We first consider the cases
!\in\{\mathrm{se},\mathrm{ex}\}
. In order to show that
F
sends
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
to equivalences it suffices to show that it admits a sequence of
factorizations

\textstyle{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{L_{h,K}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}}
\textstyle{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{F}}
\scriptstyle{L_{\mathrm{sep},!}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}}
\textstyle{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{F}}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{\mathrm{sep},!}}
\textstyle{{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,!}}^{\mathrm{group}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Since
F
is homotopy invariant and stable, it has a factorization
\bar{F}
as indicated.
The
!
-exactness of
F
implies that the functor
\bar{F}
is actually half-exact.
We now claim that
\bar{F}
sends the morphisms in the separable version
\hat{W}_{\mathrm{sep},!}

of (
5.2
) to equivalences.
In case
!=\mathrm{ex}
this is precisely
[
Bla98
, Prop. 21.4.1]
. In the case

!=\mathrm{se}
the proof of
[
Bla98
, Prop. 21.4.1]
goes though word by word since all exact sequences
used in that proof are then semi-split exact.

We now claim that
\bar{F}
also inverts the closures
W_{\mathrm{sep},!}
of
\hat{W}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
under
2
-out-of-
3

and pull-back. It suffices to show that the collection of morphisms inverted by
\bar{F}
is preserved by pull-backs.
We will use
[
Bla98
, Thm. 21.4.4]
saying that
F
admits long half-exact sequences

\cdots\to F(S(I))\to F(S(A))\xrightarrow{F(S(f))}F(S(B))\to F(I)\to F(A)\xrightarrow{F(f)}F(B)

(13.1)

associated to
exact (or semi-split exact, respectively) sequences
0\to I\to A\to B\to 0
.
For the semiexact case again note that all exact sequences appearing in the proof are semi-split exact.
Alternatively in both cases, this also follows from the half-exactness of
\bar{F}
by applying it to the image under
L_{K}
of the Puppe sequence (
3.17
) associated to the map
A\to B
.
Here we use that
F(I)\xrightarrow{\cong}F(C(f))
which follows from the fact that

F
inverts
\hat{W}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
and the analog of
5.1
for half-exact functors.

We consider the case
!=\mathrm{se}
. The case of
!=\mathrm{ex}
is simpler and obtained from the
!=\mathrm{se}
by removing all mentionings of cpc-splits.
We consider a diagram

\textstyle{L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{f}}
\textstyle{L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K}(B^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{g}}
\textstyle{L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K}(B)}

in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
.
We can assume (see e.g. the proof of
3.17
) that up to equivalence the diagram
is the image under
L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K}
of the bold part of a cartesian diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B}

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
where
f
admits cpc split.
The map
f^{\prime}
again admits a cpc split.
We can extend the vertical maps to exact sequences

0\to I\to A\xrightarrow{f}B\to 0\ ,\quad 0\to I^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}B^{\prime}\to 0

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}

such that the induced map
I\to I^{\prime}
is an isomorphism. Since
F(f)
is an isomorphism also
F(S(f))
and
F(S^{2}(f))
are isomorphisms by suspension stability.
By the long half-exact sequence (
13.1
) for
f

we conclude that
F(I)\cong 0
and
F(S(I))\cong 0
, see
13.7
. Using the version of this long half-exact sequence for
f^{\prime}
and
F(S^{2}(I^{\prime}))\cong 0
and
F(S(I^{\prime}))\cong 0
we conclude (again by
13.7
) that

F(S(f^{\prime}))
is an isomorphism.
Finally, again using suspension stability we see that
F(f^{\prime})=\bar{F}(\bar{f}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism.

We thus get a factorization
\tilde{F}
.
In
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,!}
we have the morphisms

\beta_{\mathrm{sep},!,A}:L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,!}(S^{2}(A))\to L_{\mathrm{sep},h,K,!}(A)\ .

Since
F
takes values in groups, using similar arguments as for
6.10
(replacing left-exactness by the existence of the long half-exact sequences) we know that

\tilde{F}(\beta_{\mathrm{sep},!,A})
is an isomorphism.
In detail, we consider the functor
F_{A}(-):=F(-\otimes_{\max}A)
. By

6.6
we can conclude that
F_{A}({\mathcal{T}}_{0})\cong 0
.
Then the boundary map
F_{A}(S^{2}({\mathbb{C}}))\to F_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
of the long half-exact sequence for

0\to K\to{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\to S({\mathbb{C}})\to 0
is an isomorphism.
But this map is precisely
\tilde{F}(\beta_{\mathrm{sep},!,A})
.

Consequently we get the last factorization as indicated.
This finishes the proof in the cases
!=\mathrm{se}
and
!=\mathrm{ex}
.

In the case of
!=q
we construct a sequence of factorizations

\textstyle{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{L_{h,K}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}}
\textstyle{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{F}}
\scriptstyle{L_{\mathrm{sep},!}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}}
\textstyle{L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Since
F
is split-exact and takes values in groups, by
11.4
the functor
\bar{F}
sends the morphisms

\iota^{s}_{A}:q^{s}A\to A^{s}
to isomorphisms for all
A
in
L_{K}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
.
This yields the last factorization in this case.
∎

In the following we remove the assumption of suspension stability in
13.13
.
Consider a half-exact additive category
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Proposition 13.14
.

A homotopy invariant, stable, and half-exact (or half-semiexact) functor
F:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to{\mathbf{D}}

inverts
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{ex}}
(or
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{se}}
, respectively).

Proof.

We start with the case
!=\mathrm{ex}
.
We consider the classical
E
-theory functor
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
constructed in
[
Hig90a
]
,
where we equip the additive
1
-category
\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
with the canonical half-exact structure from
13.5
.
The functor
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is homotopy invariant, stable, half-exact, and suspension stable.
In view of
13.13
the functor
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
inverts
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{ex}}
.

Let now
F:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a homotopy invariant, stable and half-exact functor.
By the universal property of
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
stated in
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.6]

we get a factorization

\textstyle{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{F}}

This implies that
F
also inverts
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{ex}}
.

We now consider the case
!=\mathrm{se}
. It would be natural to argue as in the exact case using a corresponding universal property of
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
involving half-semiexactness. But since we do not know a reference for this we
will argue differently invoking the automatic semicontinuity theorem.
Being a half-semiexact functor,
F
is in particular split-exact.
By
13.13
it inverts
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
. As a consequence of the automatic semicontinuity theorem
Theorem

12.1
we have
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},q}=\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{se}}
.
∎

bis hier

We can now state the universal property of
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
.

Proposition 13.15
.

Pull-back along
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
induces an equivalence

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{add},\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{ex}}(\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,\frac{1}{2}!}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})\ ,\quad!\in\{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{se}\}

for any additive half-exact category
{\mathbf{D}}
or

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{add}}(\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,splt}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})\ ,\quad!=q

for any additive
1
-category.

Proof.

It follows from
13.11
that the pull-back along
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
takes values in the indicated category of functors.

We first discuss the case
!\in\{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{se}\}
. We consider the diagram

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 204.14354pt\raise-34.17058pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},!}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

The lower functor is an equivalence by the universal property of the Dwyer-Kan localization
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
, see
7.5
.
The lower left equivalence is the universal property of
\mathrm{ho}
.
As a consequence we see that the functor marked by
(1)
is an equivalence.

The vertical functors are fully faithful. Therefore the functor marked by
(2)
is fully faithful, too.
It remains to show that it is essentially surjective. If
F
is any functor in

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,s,\frac{1}{2}!}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}},{\mathbf{D}})
, then by
13.14

there exists a functor
\hat{F}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!},{\mathbf{D}})
such that
\hat{F}\circ\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},!}\simeq F
.
We furthermore have a functor
\bar{F}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!},{\mathbf{D}})
such that

\mathrm{ho}^{*}\bar{F}\simeq\hat{F}
. It remains to show that

\bar{F}
is additive and half-exact.

Since
F
sends finite sums to products we can conclude that
\bar{F}
is additive.
Since all triangles in
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
come from exact (semi-split exact) sequences
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
F
is half-exact (or half-semiexact)
the functor
\bar{F}
sends the half-exact sequences in
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},!}
(with the triangulated half-exact structure)
to half-exact sequences in
{\mathbf{D}}
.
Hence
\bar{F}
is also half-exact.

In the case
!=q
we argue similarly with

47.58037pt\raise-28.49849pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.67209pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ho}^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 173.3246pt\raise-38.89912pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern ∎

Let
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
denote the classical additive category valued
functor described by the universal property
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]
.
We equip
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
with the split half-exact structure. Then

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
is split-exact.
Since
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
is also homotopy invariant and stable, by

13.13
we get a dotted factorization

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.86942pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.86942pt\raise 21.98698pt\raise 7.23112pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25891pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.73662pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.10861pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.88391pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 53.73662pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.86942pt\raise-32.21722pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 53.93301pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 47.86942pt\raise-32.21722pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.5436pt\raise-16.10861pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.8353pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

The dashed factorization is induced by the universal property of
\mathrm{ho}
since
\mathrm{KK}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is an ordinary category. Since
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
is split-exact, the dotted arrow is half-exact, and the dashed arrow is additive.

Since
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
inverts
\tilde{W}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{ex}}
(as seen in the proof of
13.14
) we also have a factorization

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.86942pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.86942pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.67804pt\raise 7.23112pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25891pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.6465pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.10861pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.52084pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.88391pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.6465pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-12.22295pt\raise-32.21722pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.86942pt\raise-32.21722pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.6465pt\raise-7.26117pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 47.86942pt\raise-32.21722pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.07846pt\raise-16.10861pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.37016pt\raise-8.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

Since
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is half-exact we can conclude that the dashed arrow
is additive and half-exact.

Theorem 13.16
.

The comparison functors

\psi:\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
and

\psi:\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
are equivalences.

Proof.

By
7.5
or
11.6
, respectively, we know that

\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
are Dwyer-Kan localizations. The composition of a Dwyer-Kan localization with the canonical functor to the homotopy category is again a localization, in this case in the sense of ordinary categories. We conclude that
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
are localizations.
Note that such a localization is determined uniquely up to equivalence under
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, and that two choices of such equivalences under
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}

are isomorphic by a unique isomorphism.

Since the universal properties of
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}

[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]

and
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}

[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.6]
are formulated in terms of equalities of functors (instead of isomorphisms),
it will be useful to choose
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
and
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{sep}}
such that these functors are bijective on objects.

We write down the details of the argument for
KK
-theory.
Since
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
is homotopy invariant, stable and split-exact,
the universal property
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]
provides an additive factorization

\textstyle{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}

which strictly commutes.

The pull-back along
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
of
the composition
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
. Therefore by

13.15
this composition
\phi\circ\psi
itself is an equivalence.

We now show that the composition
\psi\circ\phi
is also an equivalence invoking the uniqueness statement of
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]
.
This requires an equality
\psi\circ\phi\circ\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}=\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
.
By the construction of
\phi
using
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.4]
we have an equality
\phi\circ\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}=\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
.
But the construction of
\psi
only ensures a natural isomorphism

f:\psi\circ\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
which is not necessarily an equality.
We now use the freedom to replace
\psi
by an isomorphic functor and our special choice of
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
to be bijective on objects.

For every
A
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
we have an isomorphism
f_{A}:\psi(\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}(A))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}(A)
.
We define
\psi^{\prime}:\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}\to\mathrm{KK}^{\mathrm{class}}
on objects
such that
\psi^{\prime}(\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep},q}(A)):=\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}(A)
. For a morphism
h:A\to B
in
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep},q}
we then define
\psi^{\prime}(h):=f_{B}\phi(h)f_{A}^{-1}
.
The family
(f_{A})_{A}
also implements an isomorphism
\psi\cong\psi^{\prime}
.
Now
\psi^{\prime}\circ\phi\circ\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}=\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{class}}
which implies that

\psi^{\prime}\circ\phi={\mathtt{id}}
.

In particular,
\phi
has a right and a left inverse equivalence and is hence itself an equivalence.
But then also
\psi
is an equivalence.

The case of
E
-theory is completely analogous. We use the universal property
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.6]

of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
∎

Note that the proof of
Theorem

13.16
does not use the case of
13.14
for

!=\mathrm{se}
and is therefore independent of the automatic semicontinuity theorem.

14
Asymptotic morphisms in
E
-theory

The first construction of an additive
1
-category representing
E
-theory was given in
[
Hig90a
]

by enforcing universal properties. This construction was the blueprint for the
\infty
-categorical version considered in the present note.
Shortly after in
[
CH90
]
the
E
-theory groups were represented as equivalence classes of asymptotic morphisms, see also
[
GHT00
]
.
Recall that we construct
\mathrm{KK}
-theory for separable algebras
by a sequence of Dwyer-Kan localizations applied to
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
In view of
[
CH90
]
,
[
GHT00
]
a natural idea would be to apply a similar construction to the category of
C^{*}
-algebras and asymptotic morphisms.
The first obstacle one encounters in this approach is that the composition of asymptotic morphisms
is only well-defined after going over to homotopy classes. By now
3
3

3

2024
we think that the correct way to relate
E
-theory with asymptotic morphisms is the one worked out recently in
[
BD24
, Sec. 3.5]
.
It is based on the shape theory of
[
Bla85
]
,
[
Dad94
]
and goes beyond the scope of the present paper. In the present section we will therefore just show that
asymptotic morphisms also give rise to morphisms in our version
E
-theory in a way which is
compatible with the composition.

We consider the endofunctors

T,F:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

defined by

T(A):=C_{b}([0,\infty),A)\ ,\quad F(A):=C_{b}([0,\infty),A)/C_{0}([0,\infty),A)\ .

We have a natural transformation

\alpha:T\to F
such that
\alpha_{A}:T(A)\to F(A)
is the projection onto the quotient.
We furthermore have the natural transformations

\beta:{\mathtt{id}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}\to T\ ,\quad{\mathtt{ev}}_{0}:T\to{\mathtt{id}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}

such that
\beta_{A}:A\to T(A)
sends
a
in
A
to the constant function
with value
a
, and
{\mathtt{ev}}_{0,A}:T(A)\to A
evaluates the function
t\to f(t)
in
T(A)
at
t=0
. We finally define the natural transformation

\gamma:=\alpha\circ\beta:{\mathtt{id}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}\to F(A)\ .

Note that the sequence

0\to C_{0}([0,\infty),A)\to T(A)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{A}}F(A)\to 0

is exact and that

C_{0}([0,\infty),A)
is contractible. Since
\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}
is reduced, homotopy invariant and exact we see that
\mathrm{e}(\alpha_{A})
is an equivalence for every
A
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We define a natural transformation
\delta:\mathrm{e}\circ F\to\mathrm{e}
by

\delta_{A}:=\mathrm{e}({\mathtt{ev}}_{0,A})\circ\mathrm{e}(\alpha_{A})^{-1}:\mathrm{e}(F(A))\to\mathrm{e}(A)\ .

Following
[
GHT00
, Sec. 2]
we adopt the following definition.

Definition 14.1
.

For
n
in
{\rm Nature}
an asymptotic morphism
f:A\leadsto_{n}B
is a morphism
f:A\to F^{n}(B)
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Remark 14.2
.

Note that asymptotic morphisms for
n=0
are usual morphisms, and the case of
n=1
corresponds to the notion of an asymptotic morphism in
[
CH90
]
. As in
[
GHT00
, Sec. 2]

we include the case of bigger
n
in order to have a simple definition of a composition of asymptotic morphisms which also works for non-separable algebras.

∎

If
f:A\leadsto_{n}B
is an asymptotic morphism, then we define

\mathrm{e}_{n}(f):=\delta_{B}\circ\dots\circ\delta_{F^{n-1}(B)}\circ\mathrm{e}(f)\ .

If
n=0
, then this formula is interpreted as
\mathrm{e}_{0}(f):=\mathrm{e}(f)
.

Let
f^{\prime}:A\leadsto_{n+1}B
be given by
\gamma_{F^{n}(B)}\circ f
.
Then we say that
f^{\prime}
and
f
are
related
.

Lemma 14.3
.

If
f^{\prime}
is related to
f
, then
\mathrm{e}_{n}(f)\simeq\mathrm{e}_{n+1}(f^{\prime})
.

Proof.

This follows from
\mathrm{e}(\gamma_{F^{n}(B)})\simeq\mathrm{e}(\alpha_{F^{n}(B)})\circ\mathrm{e}(\beta_{F^{n}(B)})

and
\mathrm{e}({\mathtt{ev}}_{0})\circ\mathrm{e}(\beta_{F^{n}(B)})\simeq{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathrm{e}(F^{n}(B))}
.
∎

The argument implies that
\mathrm{e}_{1}(\gamma_{A})\simeq{\mathtt{id}}_{A}
for every
C^{*}
-algebra
A
.

We define the
composition
of two asymptotic morphisms

f:A\leadsto_{n}B
and
g:B\leadsto_{m}C
as

g\sharp f:A\leadsto_{n+m}C
given by
F^{n}(g)\circ f
.

Lemma 14.4
.

We have
\mathrm{e}_{n+m}(g\sharp f)\simeq\mathrm{e}_{m}(g)\circ\mathrm{e}_{n}(f)
.

Proof.

We consider the following diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.86113pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 57.53595pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 108.21077pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{e}(F^{n+m}(C))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 154.13153pt\raise-8.85721pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.64166pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{F^{m}(C)}\dots\delta_{F^{n+m-1}(C)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 197.65549pt\raise-24.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 214.21017pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 275.89159pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-32.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 36.86113pt\raise-32.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{e}(F^{n}(B))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.11945pt\raise-9.61557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{e}(F^{n}(g))}$}}}\kern 195.34218pt\raise-57.73112pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{e}_{m}(g)}$}}}\kern 214.21017pt\raise-64.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 265.84923pt\raise-64.23112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{e}(C)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

The square commutes since
\delta
is a natural transformation. The lower triangles reflect the definitions of the lower horizontal arrows.
∎

We say that
f_{0},f_{1}:A\leadsto_{n}B
are
homotopic
if there exists

f:A\leadsto_{n}C([0,1],B)
such that
F^{n}({\mathtt{ev}}_{i})\circ f=f_{i}
.

Lemma 14.5
.

If
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are homotopic, then
\mathrm{e}_{n}(f_{0})\simeq\mathrm{e}_{n}(f_{1})
.

Proof.

We have

\mathrm{e}_{n}(f_{i})\simeq\mathrm{e}_{0}({\mathtt{ev}}_{i})\sharp\mathrm{e}_{n}(f)
.
The assertion now follows since
e_{0}=e
and
e
is homotopy invariant.
∎

In the remainder of this section we relate the
E
-theory constructed in the present note with the version from

[
GHT00
]
, called the classical
E
-theory
\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{class}}
.
In
[
GHT00
, Def. 2.13]
(even in the equivariant case) a
homotopy category
\mathfrak{A}
of asymptotic morphisms
is introduced. Its objects are
C^{*}
-algebras, and its morphisms are
equivalence classes of asymptotic morphisms, where the equivalence relation is generated by the relations of being related and homotopy introduced above. The results above show that the functor
\mathrm{ho}\circ\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}
factorizes over
\mathfrak{A}
.

Corollary 14.6
.

We have a commutative triangle

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.39806pt\raise-10.26389pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ho}\circ\mathrm{e}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.96658pt\raise-25.11514pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.75002pt\raise-27.42062pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 108.64716pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-31.38889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.03671pt\raise-26.88194pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{c}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.96658pt\raise-31.38889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.35829pt\raise-31.38889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 99.96658pt\raise-31.38889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

Proof.

The down-left arrow sends a morphism
f:A\to B
to the equivalence class represented by
f\leadsto_{0}B
,
and the lower horizontal
comparison arrow

c
sends the
C^{*}
-algebra
A
to
\mathrm{e}(A)
and the class of an asymptotic morphism

f\leadsto_{n}B
to
\mathrm{e}_{n}(f)
.
∎

In
[
GHT00
, Def. 2.13]
the
classical
E
-theory category

\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{class}}
is defined
as the category whose objects are
C^{*}
-algebras, and whose morphisms are
given by

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{class}}}(A,B):={\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{A}}(K\otimes S(A),K\otimes S(B))\ .

(14.1)

It should not be confused with the separable version
E^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
from
[
Hig90a
]
.
There is a canonical functor

i:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{class}}

which is the identity on objects and
sends the class of an asymptotic morphism

f:A\leadsto_{n}B
to the class of
i(f):K\otimes S(A)\leadsto_{n}K\otimes S(B)
given by the composition

K\otimes S(A)\xrightarrow{f}K\otimes S(F^{n}(B))\to F^{n}(K\otimes S(B))\ .

(note that the second map is not an isomorphism).

Corollary 14.7
.

We have a commutative triangle

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 14.72366pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 38.72366pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.5203pt\raise-11.54611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{c}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.14859pt\raise-24.71722pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.20091pt\raise-10.73709pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.31596pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.65135pt\raise-23.17165pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-14.72366pt\raise-32.10612pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{class}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.17433pt\raise-25.49501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.61111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hat{c}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.22366pt\raise-32.10612pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.47366pt\raise-32.10612pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 76.22366pt\raise-32.10612pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(14.2)

Proof.

The lower horizontal map sends
a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
to
\mathrm{e}(A)
and the class of a morphism

f:K\otimes S(A)\to F^{n}(K\otimes S(B))
to the image under

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{A}}(K\otimes S(A),K\otimes S(B))\xrightarrow{c}\mathrm{E}_{0}(K\otimes S(A),K\otimes S(B))\cong\mathrm{E}_{0}(A,B)\ ,

where we use stability of the functor
\mathrm{e}
and stability of the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{E}
for the second isomorphism.
∎

Remark 14.8
.

The functor
\hat{c}
in (
14.2
) is not an equivalence. In fact
the classical
E
-theory functor preserves countable sums by
[
GHT00
, Prop. 7.1]
. In contrast,
the functor
\mathrm{e}
does not preserve countable sums, since
y:\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{E}
does not preserve countable sums.

But note that it is shown in
[
BD24
]
that
the restriction of
\hat{c}
to the full subcategory of separable algebras induces an equivalence

\hat{c}_{\mathrm{sep}}:\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{class}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}
. In particular the formula (
14.1
) gives an explicit description of the morphism groups in
\mathrm{ho}\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{sep}}
in terms of homotopy classes of asymptotic morphisms.

∎

Remark 14.9
.

Let

{\mathcal{S}}:\quad 0\to A\to B\to C\to 0

(14.3)

be an exact sequence of separable
C^{*}
-algebras. The
E
-theory analogue of
12.6
is

[
GHT00
, Prop. 5.5]
, where a morphism

\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}
in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{A}}(S(C),A)
was constructed.
It follows from
[
GHT00
, Prop. 5.15]
(this is an analogue of
12.14
)
that the image of
\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}
in
\mathrm{E}_{0}(S(C),A)
is the boundary map

\partial_{{\mathcal{S}}}
of the fibre sequence in
\mathrm{E}
associated to the exact sequence (
14.3
).
This shows that the comparison functor
\hat{c}
is compatible with the long exact sequences associated to exact sequences of separable
C^{*}
-algebras.

∎
